Statistically stationary solutions to the stochastic isentropic compressible Euler equations with linear damping
Jeffrey Kuan 1 , Krutika Tawri 2 and Konstantina Trivisa 1

Abstract
. We study the long time behavior of isentropic compressible Euler equations with linear damping driven by a white-in-time noise, on a one-dimensional torus. We prove the existence of a statistically stationary solution in the class of weak martingale entropy solutions for any adiabatic constant γ > 1 \gamma>1 , which satisfies an associated entropy inequality. To establish this result, we use a multi-level approximation scheme consisting of a truncation parameter R R and an artificial viscosity parameter ϵ \epsilon . The truncated system preserves the structure of the regularized system with the artificial viscosity, thereby providing key properties such as an invariant region and non-existence of vacuum at the approximate level. These properties allow us to construct an invariant measure for the approximate system in both R R and ϵ \epsilon associated to a Feller semigroup for the well-posed dynamics of the approximate system for any γ > 1 \gamma>1 . This gives us a statistically stationary solution for the approximate problem, which we then successively pass to the limit as R → ∞ R\to\infty and as ϵ → 0 \epsilon\to 0 to obtain a statistically stationary solution to the original stochastic system. Our analysis is novel, using new techniques for establishing uniform bounds on entropies of all orders, which allow us to pass to the limit in the parameters. We believe that this result is a valuable step towards further understanding the long-time statistical behavior of the stochastic Euler equations in one spatial dimension.



1.
Introduction

In this manuscript, we study the stochastic compressible Euler equations with linear damping, given by

(1)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\text{div}(\rho u)=0\\
\partial_{t}(\rho u)+\partial_{x}(\rho u^{2}+p(\rho))=\Phi(\rho,\rho u)dW-\alpha\rho u\end{cases}\quad\text{ for }(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\times\mathbb{T},

where
\mathbb{T}
is the one-dimensional torus (so that we impose periodic boundary conditions) and where
\rho
and
u
represent the density and the velocity of the compressible isentropic fluid, and hence
\rho u
represents the momentum of the fluid. The damping is linear damping in the momentum equation, where the intensity of the damping is determined by the positive constant
\alpha>0
. The constitutive relationship for the pressure in terms of the density is given by the power law:

p(\rho)=\kappa\rho^{\gamma}\text{ for }\gamma>1,

where we define the following constants in terms of
\gamma
:

\kappa=\frac{\theta^{2}}{\gamma}\quad\text{ and }\quad\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}>0.

For this system, we introduce the state variables for the fluid density
\rho
and the fluid momentum
q
:

(2)

\boldsymbol{U}:=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
q\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{ for }q:=\rho u,

as we will often work with the momentum
q
instead of the fluid velocity
u
at various points throughout the analysis. We remark that a similar system is considered in
[
4
,
35
]
in the undamped case of
\alpha=0
.

1.1.
The stochastic noise

The stochastic noise is represented by the multiplicative noise term
\Phi(\boldsymbol{U})dW
in the momentum equation. Here,
\{W(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
represents a cylindrical Wiener process with respect to a filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, taking values in a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{U}
. Letting
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
denote an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{U}
, we can represent the cylindrical Wiener process taking values in
\mathcal{U}
formally as

W(t):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}W_{k}(t)\boldsymbol{e}_{k},

where
\{W_{k}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}
for positive integers
k
is a collection of independent one-dimensional Brownian motions indexed by the positive integers
k
.

Next, we define the noise coefficient
\Phi(\boldsymbol{U}):\mathcal{U}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
by defining its action on the orthonormal basis elements
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{U}
. We define

(3)

\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{e}_{k}:=G_{k}(x,\rho,q)=\rho g_{k}(x,\rho,q),

where for each
(\rho,q)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,
g_{k}(x,\rho,q)
is a continuous periodic function on
\mathbb{T}
. We make the Lipschitz assumption on the stochastic noise that

(4)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\rho,q}g_{k}(x,\rho,q)|+|g_{k}(x,\rho,q)|\leq\alpha_{k}\qquad

\displaystyle\text{for all }(x,\rho,q)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},

for some positive constants
\alpha_{k}
such that
\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}^{2}\leq A_{0}
.

We also introduce the notation:

\boldsymbol{G}(x,\rho,q):=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|G_{k}(x,\rho,q)|^{2}\right)^{1/2},

and we note that the following bound is a direct consequence of the assumption (
4
) on the noise:

(5)

|G_{k}(x,\rho,q)|\leq\alpha_{k}\rho\quad\Longrightarrow\quad|\boldsymbol{G}(x,\rho,q)|\leq A_{0}^{1/2}\rho.

1.2.
Summary of the system

We can express the given system in quasilinear form via a single equation, by recalling the vector
\boldsymbol{U}
of state variables for the fluid density and the momentum from (
2
). We can then rewrite the system of equations in (
1
) as

(6)

d\boldsymbol{U}+\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U})dt=\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{U})dt+\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\boldsymbol{U})dW,

for an associated flux, forcing, and noise function:

\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U}):=\begin{pmatrix}q\\
\frac{q^{2}}{\rho}+p(\rho)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\boldsymbol{A}(\boldsymbol{U})=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\alpha q\\
\end{pmatrix},\qquad\boldsymbol{\Psi}(\boldsymbol{U}):=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\Phi(\boldsymbol{U})\\
\end{pmatrix}.

1.3.
The entropy inequality and weak martingale entropy solutions

If we have sufficiently smooth functions
\eta:(\rho,{u})\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
H:(\rho,{u})\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

(7)

\nabla\eta(\rho,q)\nabla\boldsymbol{F}(\rho,q)=\nabla H(\rho,q)

where the gradient is with respect to the variables
\rho
and
q
, we formally have by applying Itô’s formula with the functional
\boldsymbol{U}\to\eta(\boldsymbol{U})
that

(8)

d\eta(\boldsymbol{U})+\partial_{x}H(\boldsymbol{U})dt+\alpha q\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})dt=\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{U})dW+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U})dt,

where we refer to
({\eta},H)
satisfying the relation (
7
) as an
entropy-flux pair
. For weak entropy solutions, we expect the equation (
8
) to hold in a weak sense (distributionally) with an inequality rather than equality, to account for potential increase in entropy due to the appearance of shocks. This is what is referred to as the entropy inequality.

We will rigorously state the entropy inequality in the forthcoming definition in Definition
1.2
, which we require to hold for
all entropy-flux pairs

(\eta,H)
satisfying (
7
). However, we first discuss before stating the entropy inequality, a characterization of the specific entropy flux pairs
(\eta,H)
that we will use for the entropy inequality, satisfying the relation (
7
). Using a kinetic formulation of the system of conservation laws (see
[
35
,
36
]
), it is well-known that one can use the following explicit formula to generate entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
:

(9)

\begin{split}&\eta(\boldsymbol{U})=\rho c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}g(u+z\rho^{\theta})(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz,\\
&H(\boldsymbol{U})=\rho c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}g(u+z\rho^{\theta})(u+z\theta\rho^{\theta})(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz,\end{split}

where the constants
\theta,\lambda
and
c_{\lambda}
are defined, for
\gamma>1
, by

\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2},\qquad\lambda=\frac{3-\gamma}{2(\gamma-1)},\qquad c_{\lambda}=\left(\int_{-1}^{1}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz\right)^{-1},

and
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a function satisfying
g\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
is a convex function (
g^{\prime\prime}(z)\geq 0
for all
z
). Namely, any such
C^{2}
convex function
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
will generate an associated entropy-flux pair
(\eta,H)
where the associated entropy
\eta(\boldsymbol{U})
is a convex function of
U
, in the physically relevant region away from vacuum (namely
\rho>0
and
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
). We make the following technical assumption on the convex functions
g\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
that we use to generate the entropy-flux pairs, which is commonplace in the existing literature on compressible isentropic Euler equations
[
4
,
35
]
.

Definition 1.1
.

We define the class
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
of admissible convex functions
g\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
to be all convex functions
g\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
that are
subpolynomial
in the sense that for some constant
C
and some positive integer
m
:

(10)

|g(z)|\leq C(1+|z|^{2m}),\quad|g^{\prime}(z)|\leq C(1+|z|^{2m-1}),\quad|g^{\prime\prime}(z)|\leq C(1+|z|^{2m-2}),\quad\text{ for all }z\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Then, the classical definition of a weak martingale entropy solution to the stochastic compressible isentropic (damped) Euler equations is as follows, see
[
4
]
:

Definition 1.2
.

We say that
(\rho,q)
on a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with a filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and a
U
-valued cylindrical Wiener process
\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a
weak martingale entropy solution
to (
6
) if the following conditions are satisfied:

1.
\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-valued cylindrical Wiener process on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with respect to the filtration
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

2.
\boldsymbol{U}=(\rho,q)\in C([0,\infty);H^{-2}(\mathbb{T}))
almost surely.

3.
(\rho,q)
is locally integrable on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
and is of finite energy in the sense that for the energy
\eta_{E}(\rho,q):=\frac{1}{2}\frac{q^{2}}{\rho}+\frac{\kappa}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}
, for
\gamma>1
and for all
T>0
:

\mathbb{E}\|\eta_{E}(\boldsymbol{U})\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}<\infty.

4. The term
{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{U})
is progressively measurable with
{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{U})\in L^{2}(\Omega\times[0,T];L_{2}(\mathcal{U};L^{2}(\mathbb{T})))
for all
T>0
, where
L_{2}(\mathcal{U};L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
denotes the space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
\mathcal{U}
to
L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.

5. For all entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
arising from functions
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
, and for all
\varphi(x)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
and
non-negative

\psi(t)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,\infty)
:

(11)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})\varphi(x)dx\right)\partial_{t}\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}H(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{x}\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha q\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})\Phi(\boldsymbol{U})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dW(t)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\leq 0.

The entropy inequality (
11
) is required to hold for all entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
arising from the formulas (
9
) for all convex subpolynomial
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
. However, it will be useful to consider specific functions
g
in the entropy formula. For example, the constant function
g(z)=1
produces the associated entropy
\eta_{0}=1
, the linear function
g(z)=z
produces the momentum
\eta=\rho u
, and the quadratic function
g(z)=\frac{1}{2}z^{2}
produces the natural energy

\eta_{E}:=\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}.

In particular, we make the observation that using the entropy
\eta_{E}
in the entropy inequality (
11
) with
\varphi\equiv 1
and
\psi:=\mathbbm{1}_{[0,t]}
recovers the usual energy inequality:

\eta_{E}(\boldsymbol{U}(t))\leq\eta_{E}(\boldsymbol{U}_{0})+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi(\rho,\rho u)dx\right)dW(s)+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho^{-1}G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}(s))dx\right)ds.

Therefore, we see that the entropy inequality (
11
)
generalizes the energy inequality
, and by substituting different choices of the entropy other than
\eta_{E}
, we can obtain more information. While the formula (
9
) admits all convex
g\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
, it will be helpful to consider specific choices of
g
. In particular, it is useful to define
higher-order entropy-flux pairs

(\eta_{m},H_{m})
, which are associated with the convex functions
g(z)=z^{2m}
for nonnegative integers
m
via the formula (
9
), so that
\eta_{1}\sim 2\eta_{E}
.

Remark 1.1

(Remarks on the definition of a weak martingale entropy solution)
.

We remark that Condition 5 is called the
entropy inequality
, which is the distributional form of (
8
), expressed as an inequality to take into account the influence of shock formation on entropy. We also remark that usually, a
weak martingale (entropy) solution
is defined on an interval
[0,T]
, with
initial data

\rho(0)=\rho_{0}
and
q(0)=q_{0}
which must be satisfied. However, we state the weak martingale solution in the context of all
t\geq 0
,
t\in[0,\infty)
without initial data, as statistically stationary solutions (which will be the focus of our manuscript), do not have a notion of initial data and are also defined for all time
t\geq 0
. Namely, their main defining feature, other than satisfying the entropy inequality, is stationarity, and hence, one does not a priori provide initial data when considering stationary solutions.

Remark 1.2

(Remark on admissible entropies)
.

Note that in Condition 5, we require the entropy inequality to hold for all entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
, generated from (
9
) by subpolynomial
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
. We remark that this is a stronger condition than usually required (see for example
[
4
,
35
]
), where only entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
arising from subquadratic
g\in\mathcal{G}
are considered. However, in our case, since our statistically stationary solution will have bounded entropies of all orders, we will use the broader class arising from subpolynomial
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
for our entropy inequality. We also note here that, although not all of these higher-order entropy bounds are required for proving our main result, they are intrinsic to the system and arise naturally as a byproduct of our analysis.

1.4.
Energy dissipation and statistically stationary solutions

Assuming that
\rho(t,x)
and
u(t,x)
are smooth functions, one can show immediately that
(\rho,u)
satisfying the following energy estimate:

(12)

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho u^{2}(t)dx+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho^{\gamma}(t)dx+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho u^{2}dxds=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{\rho_{0}}+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{0}^{\gamma}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Phi(\rho,u)udx\right)dW(s),

where the natural energy associated to the problem is

E(t):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho u^{2}(t)+\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho^{\gamma}(t).

Note that in the absence of damping
(\alpha=0)
and in the absence of stochasticity, the deterministic dynamics are
isentropic
, namely energy is conserved
E(t)=E(0)
for classical solutions. However, in the case of stochasticity and damping, there are two direct contributions changes in the energy to the system encoded in the energy estimate (
12
):

•

Energy added to the system from the stochastic forcing, which has the potential to increase the energy of the system in expectation.

•

Energy, dissipated from the system as a result of the damping.

In order to obtain long-time behavior, one would expect the dynamics of the system to be bounded in long time. Namely, we would want the linear damping to dissipate energy in expectation in a way that balances out the potential energy increase due to the stochasticity. As an important step towards understanding the long-time statistical behavior of the stochastic damped Euler system (
1
), we claim that for this system, we have a
statistically stationary solution
. Heuristically, the existence of stationary solutions intuitively gives information about the long-time statistics of a stochastic dynamical system, namely one would expect that the laws of a stochastic solution in time (or the time-averaged laws) would converge weakly in long time to the law of a statistically stationary solution.

We remark that a particularly interesting question is whether such a statistically stationary solution exists in the undamped case where
\alpha=0
, in which case some other mechanism must be present to dissipate the energy added by the stochasticity. In this case, the formation of shocks heuristically should be the mechanism that can dissipate energy (which is accounted for by the fact that the energy balance in (
12
) should really be an energy inequality, in agreement with the entropy inequality of which the energy inequality is a special case). However, this problem, while fundamentally important and mathematically interesting, is beyond the scope of this manuscript.

We define the notion of a
statistically stationary solution
that we will consider in this manuscript. Intuitively, such a solution has statistics at all times that are the same.

Definition 1.3
.

A weak martingale entropy solution
(\rho,q)
to the main equation (
1
) with the stochastic basis
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},W)
(see Definition
1.2
) is a
statistically stationary solution
if
\rho\in C_{w}(\mathbb{R}^{+};L^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))
and
q\in C_{w}(\mathbb{R}^{+};L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\mathbb{T}))
almost surely and the law of
(\rho(t),q(t))
in the state space
L^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\mathbb{T})
is independent of the time
t\geq 0
. Hence,
(\rho(s),q(s))=_{d}(\rho(t),q(t))
for any
s,t\geq 0
, where
=_{d}
denotes equality in law.

We now can state the main theorem of the manuscript.

Theorem 1.1

(Main theorem)
.

Under the assumption (
4
) on the noise coefficient
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\rho,q):\mathcal{U}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, there exists a statistically stationary weak martingale entropy solution
(\rho,q)
with an associated stochastic basis
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\tilde{W})
to the damped compressible stochastic Euler equations in (
1
), for any fixed but arbitrary damping parameter
\alpha>0
.

Remark 1.3
.

Using the methodology of this work, we can obtain the existence of a statistically stationary solution in any state space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
, for any
1<p<\infty.

Remark 1.4
.

Note that the system (
1
) conserves total mass in time. So more generally, there exists at least one statistically stationary solution corresponding to every possible value of the total mass
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(t,x)dx=M>0
. For the purpose of the proof however, we will only consider
M=1
, since the generalization to arbitrary
M
is immediate.

1.5.
Significance of results and literature review

The long-time statistical behavior of stochastic physical systems is a question of inherent physical interest, in addition to being a mathematically interesting problem. Namely, in a system subject to random perturbations, individual observed outcomes of how the system evolves may appear to be disordered, yet when considering an ensemble of repeated experiments, there may be convergence of the overall statistics of the system in long time. This is a fundamental question, especially in fluid dynamics, where the long time statistical behavior of fluid flows is of particular interest for experiments and for the engineering of real-life physical systems.

The long-time statistical behavior of a stochastic system can be encoded in two ways. At a first level, one can show the existence of a
statistically stationary solution
, see for example Definition
1.3
, which has a law that is constant in time. Heuristically, one would expect the time-averaged statistics of a stochastic system to converge to the statistics of a statistically stationary solution. A stronger notion of stationarity is the
existence of an invariant measure
, which involves showing that the random dynamics for the stochastic system are well-posed (existence, uniqueness, continuous dependence). Thus, it makes sense to describe an associated Feller semigroup to the dynamics of the stochastic system, and an invariant measure, which in this case is a probability measure on the state space whose overall statistics are unchanged by the dynamics of the random system.

The question of long-time statistical behavior of stochastic fluid systems is classical, tracing back to results on existence of statistically stationary solutions and invariant measures for the stochastic incompressible Navier-Stokes equations. In 2D, the Navier-Stokes equations have global existence and uniqueness in the deterministic case, which allows for analysis of invariant measures for the 2D stochastic Navier-Stokes equations
[
10
,
11
,
21
,
26
,
28
,
29
,
31
,
32
,
37
,
38
,
41
]
. This includes work on existence/uniqueness of invariant measures for 2D Navier-Stokes equations with discrete-in-time random “kick” forces
[
10
,
31
,
32
,
37
]
and more general noise (such as white noise in time or cylindrical Wiener processes)
[
11
,
21
,
26
,
28
,
29
,
38
,
41
]
, and work on exponential mixing and exponential convergence of statistics
[
11
,
28
,
37
,
38
]
. These results were extended to (the weaker notion of) statistically stationary solutions in 3D
[
25
,
27
,
39
,
40
]
, but invariant measures are in general still an open problem due to the lack of well-posedness for 3D Navier-Stokes, though some approaches are able to construct transition semigroups for dynamics even in the absence of uniqueness
[
27
,
39
,
40
]
.

The extension of the analysis of long-time behavior to the case of stochastic compressible fluids is more recent. This work was made possible first from new existence results for weak solutions to stochastic compressible Navier-Stokes equations for compressible viscous fluids
[
7
,
8
,
44
,
45
]
, which were natural stochastic extensions of deterministic existence results for global weak solutions in the spirit of Leray-Hopf for (deterministic) compressible Navier-Stokes equations
[
23
,
34
]
. The work
[
23
]
on the deterministic compressible Navier-Stokes equations involves a four-layer approximation scheme including an artificial pressure parameter
\delta
and an artificial viscosity parameter
\epsilon
. This methodology has been robust, and has been used to analyze existence of global in time weak martingale solutions to stochastic compressible flows
[
7
,
8
,
44
,
45
]
.

These developments in the
existence theory
for stochastic compressible fluid flows then led to the study of the
long-time behavior
of stochastic compressible viscous fluid flows. In fact, a similar four-layer approximation scheme was used to show the existence of a statistically stationary solution to the stochastic compressible Navier-Stokes dynamics in
[
9
]
, where an invariant measure for an approximate system is constructed and is passed through the various approximation layers. We remark however that the proof of existence of a statistically stationary solution is much more delicate than the existence proof, since one must obtain uniform-in-time estimates for the approximate system, and in addition, one can appeal only to stationarity to obtain uniform estimates at each approximation level. Namely, stationary solutions have no “initial data”, and thus one can only appeal to stationarity to define any uniform estimates. A stronger result on existence of an invariant measure was obtained for the specific case of 1D compressible Navier-Stokes equations, with a barotropic fluid for linear pressure law and a linear pressure law in
[
46
]
. The challenge in this work is establishing well-posedness (existence, uniqueness, continuous dependence on initial data) for the stochastic compressible Navier-Stokes system in 1D, which involves careful a priori estimates. This well-posedness is necessary for defining an associated Feller semigroup for the stochastic compressible Navier-Stokes dynamics, which is needed to properly define a notion of invariant measure.

These results on existence of stochastic solutions and long-time statistics discussed so far have been for compressible
viscous
fluids, but there have not been many developments in the study of stochastic compressible inviscid fluids.
For a study of statistically stationary solutions or invariant measures to the incompressible Euler equations with linear damping, see
[
6
,
5
]
and for fractionally dissipated Euler equations, see
[
15
]
.
In the context of inviscid
compressible
fluid flows, global existence of weak martingale entropy solutions, namely stochastic solutions that satisfy not just a weak formulation but a general entropy inequality, was accomplished in the work
[
4
]
. This work uses important techniques from the deterministic study of inviscid compressible flows (most importantly
[
35
]
, where existence of weak entropy solutions is established for isentropic Euler equations in 1D, and related works
[
13
,
18
,
19
,
36
]
) and invariant regions
[
14
]
(see also
[
20
,
19
]
for applications to deterministic isentropic Euler equations), to obtain uniform estimates on approximate solutions and to pass to the limit in an artificial viscosity parameter using Young measure compactness results and then reducing Young measures to a Dirac mass due to the presence of a large family of
entropies. While some numerical evidence is provided in
[
4
]
to support the existence of an invariant measure for the 1D stochastic inviscid isentropic Euler equations, to the best of our knowledge, progress has not been made on this question. Hence, we believe that the result of the current manuscript, namely the existence of a statistically stationary solution to the
damped
1D stochastic compressible Euler equations, is a significant step towards the eventual goal of showing invariant measures to the undamped stochastic compressible Euler equations.

1.6.
Algebraic bounds on the
m
-order entropies

In this section we will find algebraic bounds for the entropy functions
\eta_{m}
, defined in (
9
) arising from polynomial
g(z)=z^{2m}
, in terms of density
\rho
and velocity
u
.

Proposition 1.1
.

We have the following bounds for
\eta_{m}
, where the constants
c_{m}
and
C_{m}
depend only on
m
:

c_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{m(\gamma-1)})\leq\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\leq C_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{m(\gamma-1)}),

and in addition, for some positive constants
c_{m}
and
C_{m}
:

c_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}u^{2})\leq q\partial_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\leq C_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}u^{2}),

\partial_{q}^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\leq C_{m}\rho^{-2}\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U}).

Proof.

Recall that
\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
.
We obtain by substituting
g(z)=z^{2m}
into (
9
) and the binomial theorem that

\displaystyle\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})

\displaystyle=\rho c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}(u+z\rho^{\theta})^{2m}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz=c_{\lambda}\rho\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}z^{2m-j}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz\right){C^{m}_{j}}u^{j}\rho^{(2m-j)\theta}

(13)

\displaystyle=c_{\lambda}\rho\sum_{j=0}^{m}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}z^{2(m-j)}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz\right){C^{m}_{j}}u^{2j}\rho^{2(m-j)\theta}:=\sum_{j=0}^{m}a_{m-j}{C^{m}_{j}}u^{2j}\rho^{1+2(m-j)\theta},

where we define the (strictly positive) coefficients
\displaystyle a_{k}:=c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}z^{2k}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz
. From the expansion (
1.6
), we immediately deduce that
\eta_{m}(U)\geq\min(a_{0},a_{m})\cdot\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{2m\theta})
, and by using Young’s inequality with exponents
m/j
and
m/(m-j)
applied to the terms
u^{2j}
and
\rho^{2(m-j)\theta}
in (
1.6
), we also obtain
\eta_{m}(U)\leq C_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{2m\theta})
. We can rewrite (
1.6
) in terms of
(\rho,q)
using
q=\rho u
, as
\displaystyle\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})=\sum_{j=0}^{m}a_{m-j}q^{2j}\rho^{1+2(m-j)\theta-2j}
, and hence, using Young’s inequality:

q\partial_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}2ja_{m-j}\rho^{1+2(m-j)\theta}u^{2j}\leq C_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{2(m-1)\theta}u^{2}),

\partial_{q}^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})=\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}(2j+2)(2j+1)a_{m-j-1}\rho^{2((m-1)-j)\theta+1}u^{2j}\leq C_{m}\rho^{-2}\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U}).

∎

It will also be important to deduce certain algebraic bounds on the higher moments of the state variables
\boldsymbol{U}=(\rho,q)
. For this, we recall the following algebraic identities that bound higher powers of the density and momentum in terms of the higher entropies
\eta_{m}
corresponding to
g(z)=z^{2m}
. This will allow us to relate bounds on the higher-order entropies to higher integrability (
L^{s}
norms) of the lower-order entropies. The following lemma is from Lemma 3.12 from
[
4
]
.

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
(\eta_{m},H_{m})
denote the entropy-flux pair corresponding to the convex function
g(z)=z^{2m}
for nonnegative integers
m
. Then, for any
s\geq 1
,

|\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})|^{s}\leq C(m,s,p)\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{p}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big)\quad\text{ for }p\geq ms+\frac{s-1}{(\gamma-1)},

|H_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})|^{s}\leq C(m,s,p)\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{p}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big)\quad\text{ for }p\geq\left(m+\frac{1}{2}\right)s+\frac{s-1}{(\gamma-1)},

for a constant
C(m,s,p)
depending only on
s\geq 1
, and the nonnegative integers
m
and
p
.

Finally, we will establish the following uniform algebraic bound on the entropy dissipation.

Proposition 1.2
.

Suppose that we have that for some deterministic
\boldsymbol{U}:=(\rho,q)
which is a function from
\mathbb{T}
to
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
and for some nonnegative integer
m
:

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}\rangle dx\leq C.

Then, for a constant
C_{m}
depending only on
m
:

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(u^{2m}+\rho^{2m\theta}\Big)\rho^{\gamma-2}(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}dx\leq C_{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}\rangle dx,

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(u^{2m}+\rho^{2m\theta}\Big)\rho(\partial_{x}u)^{2}dx\leq C_{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}\rangle dx.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the algebraic computations in Proposition 3.14 and Corollary 3.15 in
[
4
]
, if one keeps track of the constants in the proofs.
∎

1.7.
Algebraic bounds on general entropy functions

The entropy inequality (
11
) is required to hold for all general entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
generated by general convex functions
g\in C^{2}({\mathbb{R}})
via (
11
) which are in
\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
. However, because of the subpolynomial bound on admissible functions
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
in (
10
), a general entropy can be bounded above by the entropies of the form
\eta_{m}
that we defined for the special functions
g(z)=z^{2m}
. In contrast to the previous Section
1.6
which derives entropy bounds specifically for entropy functions of the form
\eta_{m}
, we derive some algebraic bounds on
general
entropies
\eta
(for arbitrary
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
) in terms of the special entropies
\eta_{m}
in this section, to justify this reasoning.

First, we derive a bound on the general entropies
\eta
for
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
under the assumption of bounded densities. This will be useful for passing to the limit in approximation levels where we still have some uniform control over the maximum value of the density.

Proposition 1.3
.

For a general
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
satisfying (
10
) for some positive integer
m
, there exists a constant
C_{g,M}
depending only on
g
and
M
such that for all
(\rho,q)
satisfying:

0<\rho\leq M,\qquad\left|\frac{q}{\rho}\right|\leq M

for some positive constant
M>0
, we have the bounds:

(14)

|\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g,M}\rho,\qquad|\nabla_{\rho,q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g,M},\qquad\rho|\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g,M}.

Proof.

For
\displaystyle\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
we compute the first derivatives of the entropy using (
9
) as follows:

\partial_{\rho}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})=c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}g\left(\frac{q}{\rho}+z\rho^{\theta}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz+c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}g^{\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\rho}+z\rho^{\theta}\right)\left(\theta z\rho^{\theta}-\frac{q}{\rho}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz,

(15)

\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})=c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}g^{\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\rho}+z\rho^{\theta}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz.

For the second derivatives of the entropy, we compute:

\partial_{\rho}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})=c_{\lambda}\theta(1-\theta)\rho^{\theta-1}\int_{-1}^{1}g^{\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\rho}+z\rho^{\theta}\right)z(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz+c_{\lambda}\rho\int_{-1}^{1}g^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\rho}+z\rho^{\theta}\right)\left(\theta z\rho^{\theta-1}-\frac{q}{\rho^{2}}\right)^{2}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz.

\partial_{q}\partial_{\rho}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})=c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}g^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\rho}+z\rho^{\theta}\right)\left(\theta z\rho^{\theta-1}-\frac{q}{\rho^{2}}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz

(16)

\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})=c_{\lambda}\rho^{-1}\int_{-1}^{1}g^{\prime\prime}\left(\frac{q}{\rho}+z\rho^{\theta}\right)(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz.

Using the fact that
g(z),g^{\prime}(z),g^{\prime\prime}(z)
are continuous functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
, and hence are bounded on compact subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}
, we have that the inequalities (
14
) follow immediately.
∎

For later approximation level passages, where we do not have uniform bounds on the maximum of the density, we have the following more general bound.

Proposition 1.4
.

Suppose that
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
satisfies (
10
) for some positive integer
m
. Then, for some constant
C_{g}
depending on
g
and for all
\boldsymbol{U}:=(\rho,q)\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
:

(17)

|\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big)\quad\text{ and }\quad|H(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big).

Proof.

Using (
9
) and the subpolynomial bound (
10
) for
g
and
\displaystyle\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
we have,

\displaystyle|\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|

\displaystyle\leq C\rho\int_{-1}^{1}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz+C\rho\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}|z|^{2m-j}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz\right)C^{2m}_{j}|u|^{j}\rho^{(2m-j)\theta}

\displaystyle\leq C\rho+C\rho\sum_{j=0}^{2m}b_{2m-j}C^{2m}_{j}|u|^{j}\rho^{(2m-j)\theta},

where
C^{m}_{j}
are binomial coefficients and
\displaystyle b_{2m-j}:=\int_{-1}^{1}|z|^{2m-j}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz
. Using Young’s inequality with exponents
2m/j
and
2m/(2m-j)
, we obtain that,

|\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C\rho+C\rho\Big(u^{2m}+\rho^{2m\theta}\Big)=C\rho+C\rho\Big(u^{2m}+\rho^{m(\gamma-1)}\Big)\leq C\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),

where we used the fact that
\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})=\rho
, and the bounds in Proposition
1.1
. Similarly, for
H(\boldsymbol{U})
, corresponding to
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
satisfying (
10
), we estimate using (
10
) and the formula in (
9
) that

\displaystyle|H(\boldsymbol{U})|

\displaystyle\leq\rho c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}(|u|+|z|\theta\rho^{\theta})(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz+C_{\lambda,\theta}\rho\int_{-1}^{1}\Big(|u|+|z\rho^{\theta}|\Big)^{2m+1}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\rho|u|+\rho^{\frac{\gamma+1}{2}}\Big)+C_{\lambda,\theta}\rho\sum_{j=0}^{2m+1}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}|z|^{2m+1-j}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz\right)|u|^{j}\rho^{(2m+1-j)\theta}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\rho+\rho|u|+\rho^{\gamma}\Big)+C\rho\Big(|u|^{2m+1}+\rho^{(2m+1)\theta}\Big),

by a similar Young’s inequality argument. Since
\theta=\frac{\gamma-1}{2}
, we have by Young’s inequality that

\rho|u|\leq C\Big(\rho+\rho|u|^{2m}\Big),\qquad\rho\Big(|u|^{2m+1}+\rho^{(2m+1)\theta}\Big)\leq C\rho\Big(1+|u|^{2(m+1)}+\rho^{(m+1)(\gamma-1)}\Big),

which gives the bound for
H(\boldsymbol{U})
in (
17
) via Proposition
1.1
.

∎

We also obtain similar algebraic bounds for other terms involving entropies that will appear in the weak formulation.

Proposition 1.5
.

Suppose that
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
satisfies (
10
) for some positive integer
m
. Then, for some constant
C_{g}
depending on
g
and for all
\boldsymbol{U}:=(\rho,q)\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
:

\displaystyle|q\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),\qquad|G(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),

\displaystyle|G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big).

Proof.

This proceeds similarly to the previous proof of Proposition
1.4
, except we use (
15
) and (
16
). Using the bounds on
|g^{\prime}(z)|
and
|g^{\prime\prime}(z)|
in (
10
), the binomial theorem, and Young’s inequality:

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\lambda}\int_{-1}^{1}|g^{\prime}(u+z\rho^{\theta})|(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}+C\sum_{j=0}^{2m-1}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}|z|^{2m-1-j}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz\right)C^{2m-1}_{j}|u|^{j}\rho^{(2m-1-j)\theta}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(1+|u|^{2m-1}+\rho^{(2m-1)\theta}\Big).

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\lambda}\rho^{-1}\int_{-1}^{1}|g^{\prime\prime}(u+z\rho^{\theta})|(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz

\displaystyle\leq C\rho^{-1}\int_{-1}^{1}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}+C\rho^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{2m-2}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}|z|^{2m-1-j}(1-z^{2})^{\lambda}dz\right)C^{2m-1}_{j}|u|^{j}\rho^{(2m-1-j)\theta}

\displaystyle\leq C\rho^{-1}\Big(1+|u|^{2m-2}+\rho^{(2m-2)\theta}\Big).

We then compute using Young’s inequality, the bound (
5
) on
G(\boldsymbol{U})
, and Proposition
1.1
that

|q\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C\rho\Big(|u|+|u|^{2m}+\rho^{(2m-1)\theta}|u|\Big)\leq C\rho\Big(1+|u|^{2m}+\rho^{2m\theta}\Big)\leq C\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),

|G(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C\rho\Big(1+|u|^{2m-1}+\rho^{(2m-1)\theta}\Big)\leq C\rho\Big(1+|u|^{2m}+\rho^{2m\theta}\Big)\leq C\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),

|G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C\rho\Big(1+|u|^{2m-2}+\rho^{(2m-2)\theta}\Big)\leq C\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big).

∎

2.
The approximate system

In this section, we define an approximate system for which we can find an
invariant measure
using a standard tightness and time-averaging procedure. For an invariant measure to exist for the approximate system, we require strong well-posedness properties for the approximate system: namely existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence. Hence, we will have to use a sufficient number of approximations and truncations in order to have a suitable approximate system. For this, we use two parameters: (1) a truncation parameter
R
and (2) an artificial viscosity parameter
\epsilon
. With each parameter, we also appropriately regularize the noise coefficients, defined in (
3
), in order to obtain high-order derivative estimates for the approximate solutions. Moreover, the noise coefficient approximation is also compactly supported which lets us obtain uniform
L^{\infty}
bounds for the approximate solutions. In particular, we consider a regularized noise coefficient
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon_{N}}(\boldsymbol{U}_{R,\epsilon}):\mathcal{U}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, where analogously to (
3
), we now define

\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon_{N}}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{e}_{k}:=G_{k}^{R,\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q)=\rho g_{k}^{R,\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q),

and we assume that, compared to (
4
), the noise satisfies stronger assumptions, which we will discuss in Section
2.2
. Here, we note that to define the regularized noise, it will be easier to consider a discrete sequence of artificial viscosity parameters
\{\epsilon_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
with
\epsilon_{N}\searrow 0
, which is why we use the notation of
\epsilon_{N}
in the approximate system.
Often, for ease of notation, we will omit the explicit sequence dependence on
N
in the sequence
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
, and just use the parameter
\epsilon>0
.

We will first state the approximate system in Section
2.1
. Then, we define the noise coefficient approximations in Section
2.2
, and we establish Hadamard well-posedness for the approximate system in Sections
2.3
and
2.4
.

2.1.
Statement of the approximate system.

Let
\chi_{R}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be a smooth function such that
\chi_{R}(s)=1
for
s\leq\frac{R}{2}
,
{\chi_{R}(s)}=0
for
s>R
, and
\chi
is strictly decreasing on the interval
[\frac{R}{2},R]
. For technical reasons, we also choose
\chi_{R}
so that
\sqrt{\chi_{R}}
is also smooth and compactly supported on
{\mathbb{R}}
(see the proof of Lemma
2.2
, where this assumption is used). Furthermore, given spatial functions
q\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and
\rho\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, define the truncation

(18)

[q]_{R}:=\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)q.

At times, for shorthand, we will use the abbreviation

(19)

\chi_{R}(\rho,q):=\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

so that

[q]_{R}=\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q.

Then, we consider the following approximate system for
(t,x)\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{T}
:

(20)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{x}([q]_{R})=\epsilon\Delta\rho,\\
\displaystyle dq+\partial_{x}\left(\frac{[q]_{R}q}{\rho}\right)+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}(\kappa\rho^{\gamma})=\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)dW-\alpha q+\epsilon\Delta q.\end{cases}

The goal will be to show existence of an invariant measure on an appropriate phase space to this system. This will involve showing well-posedness (existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence), and obtaining uniform bounds for the fluid density and fluid velocity uniformly in time.

2.2.
Approximations of the noise coefficient

We now discuss the noise coefficients and their approximations at the various levels.

Original noise coefficient.
We recall from (
3
) and (
4
) that the noise coefficient
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{U}):\mathcal{U}\to L^{2}(\mathbb{T})
acts on orthonormal basis elements
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{U}
via

\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{e}_{k}:=G_{k}(x,\rho,q)=\rho g_{k}(x,\rho,q),

where for each positive integer
k
and for each
(\rho,q)\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
g_{k}(x,\rho,q)
is a continuous real-valued function on
\mathbb{T}
. We have the growth assumption:

|g_{k}(x,\rho,q)|+|\nabla_{\rho,q}g_{k}(x,\rho,q)|\leq\alpha_{k},\qquad\text{ for all }(x,\rho,q)\in\mathbb{T}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},

for some constants
\alpha_{k}
satisfying:

(21)

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{k}^{2}=A_{0}<\infty.

Hence,

|\boldsymbol{G}(x,\rho,q)|\leq A_{0}^{1/2}\rho,\qquad\text{ for }\boldsymbol{G}(x,\rho,q):=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|G_{k}(x,\rho,q)|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

The regularized
\epsilon
-level noise coefficient.
At the
\epsilon
level, the goal is to localize the noise coefficient so that it is compactly supported on some invariant region
\Lambda_{\kappa}
for some
\kappa
related to
\epsilon>0
. For the
\epsilon
-level approximation, we will hence truncate the noise so that it is compactly supported in an invariant region. We define the following sets that will later be important as invariant regions for the approximate system (see Section
3
):

(22)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\kappa}:=

\displaystyle\{(\rho,u)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}:-\kappa\leq z\leq w\leq\kappa\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(\rho,u)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}:0\leq\rho\leq\kappa^{1/\theta},-\kappa+\rho^{\theta}\leq u\leq\kappa-\rho^{\theta}\},

and

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa}:=\{(\rho,q)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}:0\leq\rho\leq\kappa^{1/\theta},-\rho(\kappa-\rho^{\theta})\leq q\leq\rho(\kappa-\rho^{\theta})\},

where we note that
(\rho,u)\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
if and only if
(\rho,q)\in\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa}
. Note that just for the current construction of the regularized noise, we will distinguish between the sets
\Lambda_{\kappa}
in the
(\rho,u)
plane and the sets
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa}
in the
(\rho,q)
plane, but we will later denote both by
\Lambda_{\kappa}
for notational simplicity.

The goal will be to localize the noise coefficient
\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\rho,q)
to the sets
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa}
in the
(\rho,q)
state space, on the
\epsilon
level. To define the localization of the noise on these sets, we define the following compactly supported function. Let
T(x,y):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a compactly supported function such that

(23)

T\text{ is smooth, radially symmetric, and strictly decreasing radially on $1/2\leq|(x,y)|\leq 1$},

T(x,y)=1\text{ for }|(x,y)|\leq 1/2,\quad T(x,y)=0\text{ for }|(x,y)|\geq 1.

For each positive integer
N
, define the open ball of radius
2N
centered at
(\rho,q)=(2N+1/N,0)
:

(24)

B_{N}:=\{(\rho,q)\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}:\|(\rho,q)-(2N+1/N,0)\|\leq 2N\},

and note that these open ball
\{B_{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}}
increase to all of
(0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
. There exists an associated increasing sequence of positive real numbers
\{\kappa_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
such that

(25)

B_{N}\subset\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa_{N}}.

We then define the following localized noise coefficient functions, with support in
\overline{B_{N}}
:

(26)

\begin{split}&g_{k}^{\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q)=T\Big(N^{-1}(\rho-(2N+1/N),q)\Big)g_{k}(x,\rho,q),\\
&\text{ and hence }\ \ \boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{e}_{k}:=G^{\epsilon_{N}}_{k}(x,\rho,q)=\rho g^{\epsilon}_{k}(x,\rho,q).\end{split}

for any sequence of positive
\{\epsilon_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
strictly decreasing to zero as
N\to\infty
.

Importantly, the regularized noise coefficient has the following essential property, due to (
25
):

(27)

\text{supp}\Big(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q)\Big)\subset\mathbb{T}\times\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa_{N}},\qquad\text{ for all positive integers $N$}.

This is because by construction,

(28)

\text{supp}\Big(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q)\Big)\subset\mathbb{T}\times\tilde{B}_{N},

where

(29)

\tilde{B}_{N}:=\{(\rho,q)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}:\|(\rho,q)-(2N+1/N,0)\|\leq N\}\subset B_{N}\subset\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa_{N}}.

We remark that sometimes, we will will be imprecise with our notation and denote the property (
27
) by:

\text{supp}\Big(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q)\Big)\subset\mathbb{T}\times\Lambda_{\kappa_{N}},

in terms of the invariant region
\Lambda_{\kappa_{N}}
in the
(\rho,u)
plane, as we actually mean that the values of
(\rho,u)
, and not the values of
(\rho,q)
, which are in the support of
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q)
lie in
\Lambda_{\kappa}
.

We can verify from the formula (
26
) and the properties of the compactly supported function
T
in (
23
), that we still have the following properties:

|g_{k}^{\epsilon}(x,\rho,q)|\leq\alpha_{k},\qquad|\nabla_{\rho,q}g^{\epsilon}_{k}(x,\rho,q)|\leq\alpha_{k}(1+N^{-1}),

for the same constants
\alpha_{k}
as in (
4
), and hence, for the same constant
A_{0}
in (
21
):

(30)

|\boldsymbol{G}^{\epsilon}(x,\rho,q)|\leq A_{0}^{1/2}\rho,\qquad\text{ for }\boldsymbol{G}^{\epsilon}(x,\rho,q):=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|G_{k}^{\epsilon}(x,\rho,q)|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

Furthermore, using the fact that the region
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\kappa_{N}}
is bounded in the
(\rho,q)
plane depending on
\kappa_{N}
, we hence have

(31)

|G^{\epsilon}_{k}(x,\rho,q)|+|\nabla_{\rho,q}G^{\epsilon}_{k}(x,\rho,q)|\leq C_{N}\alpha_{k},

for a constant
C_{N}
depending only on
N
.

The regularized
\epsilon
-
R
noise coefficient.
At the
R
level, we additionally regularize the
\epsilon
-level noise coefficient via convolution, so that the
\epsilon
-
R
level approximations of the noise coefficients have spatial derivatives of all orders, which will help us show well-posedness for our
\epsilon
-
R
approximate system. For this approximation, let
\overline{\zeta}(z):{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a standard smooth nonnegative convolution kernel with support in
[-1,1]
with integral equal to one. Then, define

\zeta_{\alpha}(x,\rho,q)=\frac{1}{\alpha^{3}}\overline{\zeta}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)\overline{\zeta}\left(\frac{\rho}{\alpha}\right)\overline{\zeta}\left(\frac{q}{\alpha}\right).

Define for positive integers
R\geq 1
:

(32)

G^{R,\epsilon_{N}}_{k}(x,\rho,u)=G^{\epsilon_{N}}_{k}(x,\rho,u)*\zeta_{N/R}1_{k\leq R},

for positive integers
R
. Since
G^{\epsilon_{N}}_{k}(x,\rho,q)
is compactly supported on
\mathbb{T}\times\tilde{B}_{N}
where
\tilde{B}_{N}
as in (
29
) is the ball of radius
N
centered at
(2N+1/N,0)
, we note that this convolution is well-defined (extend
G^{R_{\epsilon}}_{k}(x,\rho,q)
by zero for
\rho\leq 0
) and

\text{supp}\Big(G^{R,\epsilon_{N}}_{k}(x,\rho,q)\Big)\subset\mathbb{T}\times B_{N},

where
B_{N}
is the ball of radius
2N
centered at
(2N+1/N,0)
, as in (
24
). Then, define

\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon_{N}}(x,\rho,q)\boldsymbol{e}_{k}=G^{R,\epsilon_{N}}_{k}(x,\rho,q),

and note that

(33)

\text{supp}\Big(\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(x,\rho,q)\Big)\subset\mathbb{T}\times\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}},

by (
25
). Then, for each positive integer
m
, there exists a constant
C(m,R,N)
such that

(34)

|\nabla_{\rho,q}^{m}G^{R,\epsilon}_{k}(x,\rho,q)|\leq C(m,R,N)\alpha_{k}.

By (
31
) and the properties of the convolution in (
32
), note that this constant is independent of
R
when
m=0,1
, namely:

(35)

|G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(x,\rho,q)|+|\nabla_{\rho,q}G^{R,\epsilon}_{k}(x,\rho,q)|\leq C_{N}\alpha_{k},

for a constant
C_{N}
depending only on
N
. Hence, for the same constant
A_{0}
in (
21
):

(36)

|\boldsymbol{G}^{R,\epsilon}(x,\rho,q)|\leq A_{0}^{1/2}\rho,\qquad\text{ for }\boldsymbol{G}^{R,\epsilon}(x,\rho,q):=\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(x,\rho,q)|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

2.3.
Existence and uniqueness for the approximate system and preliminary estimates

To show existence of an invariant measure for the approximate system (
20
), we will need a notion of Hadamard well-posedness for this system (global existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence). In this subsection, we consider existence and uniqueness of the approximate system (
20
) for initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})
satisfying
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{0}dx=1
with
\rho>0
, in terms of the following phase space:

(37)

\mathcal{X}:=\left\{(\rho,q)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{T}):\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(x)dx=1\text{ and }\rho\geq\frac{1}{R}\right\},

where the function space
\mathcal{X}
is endowed with the usual norm of
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
. We verify that with respect to this phase space
\mathcal{X}
, we have existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence for the approximate system (
20
), which will allow us to form a Feller semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{t}
(see Section
2.5
) for the evolution of solutions to (
20
). We first show the following existence and uniqueness result.

Proposition 2.1
.

Given
\boldsymbol{U}_{0}=(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},{\mathbb{P}})
along with a filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
-Wiener process
W
, there exists a unique (classical) pathwise solution
\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon}=(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
to the approximate problem (
20
) in the class
{L^{2}}(\Omega;C(0,{\infty};\mathcal{X}))
that satisfies, for all nonnegative test functions
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,\infty)
, and all entropy-entropy flux pair
(\eta,H)
, the following equation
{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely:

(38)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon}(t))\varphi(x)dx\right)\partial_{t}\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}H(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\\
-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha q^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dW(t)\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)=\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon}\rangle\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\\
-\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}^{R,\epsilon})\partial^{2}_{x}\varphi dx\right)\psi(t)dt.

Proof.

The proof is identical to that of Theorem 3.2 in
[
4
]
.
∎

Next, we verify some useful preliminary estimates on the global unique solution to the approximate system, which will be useful for the upcoming proof of continuous dependence. Specifically, we prove the following preliminary a priori estimate for the density.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
be the unique solution to (
20
) in
C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
for initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
. Then,
for some (deterministic) constant
C_{R,\epsilon,T}
depending only on
R
,
\epsilon
, and the final time
T
, we have the following almost sure bound:

\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}^{2}\leq\|\rho_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+C_{R,\epsilon,T}.

Proof.

By taking zero, one, and two spatial derivatives of the continuity equation in (
63
), we obtain for
i=0,1,2
:

\partial_{t}(\partial_{x}^{i}\rho^{R,\epsilon})+\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{i+1}q^{R,\epsilon}=\epsilon\partial_{x}^{i+2}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\qquad\text{ on }[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}.

By testing with
\partial_{x}^{i}\rho^{R,\epsilon}
and integrating by parts, we obtain:

\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}^{i}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}(t)+\epsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{i+1}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}^{i}\rho_{0})^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial^{i}_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{i+1}\rho^{R,\epsilon}.

By estimating

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{i}q^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{i+1}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}^{i+1}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}+C(\epsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{i}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial^{i+1}_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}+C(\epsilon,R)t,

since
\|\chi(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})q^{R,\epsilon}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq R
by the definition of the truncation in (
19
). Thus,

\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}^{i}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}(t)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{i+1}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\partial_{x}^{i}\rho_{0})^{2}+C(\epsilon,R)t

almost surely, from which we obtain the desired (almost sure) estimate from Gronwall’s inequality.

∎

We also have the following minimum principle for initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
, which we recall from the definition of the phase space
\mathcal{X}
in (
37
) must satisfy
\rho_{0}\geq\frac{1}{R}
. This result is important because it shows that the unique solution
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
to (
20
) in
C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
for initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
is more specifically in
C(0,T;\mathcal{X})
since by the definition of the phase space
\mathcal{X}
in (
37
), functions
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
in
\mathcal{X}
must satisfy the pointwise lower bound for the density
\rho^{R,\epsilon}\geq\frac{1}{R}
. This also importantly gives us uniform control in time on the density
\rho^{R,\epsilon}
away from vacuum.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
\rho^{R,\epsilon}\in C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
and
q^{R,\epsilon}\in C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
satisfy

(39)

\partial_{t}\rho^{R,\epsilon}+\partial_{x}([q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R})=\epsilon\Delta\rho^{R,\epsilon},\qquad\text{ on }\mathbb{T},

for initial data
\rho(0)=\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
. Then,
\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,\cdot)\geq\frac{1}{R}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, that is we have
\rho^{R,\epsilon}\in C(0,T;\mathcal{X})
.

Proof.

Note that the initial data
\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{X}
. Now observe that (
20
)
1
can be written as

(40)

\partial_{t}(\rho^{R,\epsilon}-R^{-1})+\partial_{x}([q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R})=\epsilon\Delta(\rho^{R,\epsilon}-R^{-1}).

First, we claim for any
t\in[0,T]
, that

\chi_{R}\Big(\|(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t))^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\cdot\text{sgn}^{+}(R^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t))=0
.
Here
\text{sgn}^{+}(x)=1
if
x\geq 0
and is equal to 0 if
x<0
.
Notice that this claimed equality is true if for all times we have
\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t)>-\frac{1}{R}
.
Hence, for a contradiction, we assume that for a fixed but arbitrary
\omega\in\Omega
there exists a time
\tau(\omega)<T
such that
\tau(\omega)
is the first time when
\rho^{R,\epsilon}(\tau(\omega))=-\frac{1}{R}
and hence the first time when
\chi_{R}\Big(\|(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(\tau))^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\cdot\text{sgn}^{+}(R^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon}(\tau))\neq 0
. Note that since
\rho^{R,\epsilon}\in C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
and since
\rho_{0}\geq\frac{1}{R}
, we must have
0<\tau(\omega)
for every
\omega\in\Omega
.

Now we multiply (
40
) by
\text{sgn}^{+}({R}^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon})
and integrate in space and then in time on
[0,\tau(\omega)]
to obtain:

\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\rho^{R,\epsilon}-R^{-1})^{-}(\tau)=\epsilon\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Delta(R^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon})\cdot\text{sgn}^{+}(R^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon}),

since
\rho_{0}\geq 1/R
, so that
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\rho_{0}-R^{-1})^{-}=0
, and since by assumption

\chi_{R}\Big(\|(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\cdot\text{sgn}^{+}(R^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon})=0
for
t<\tau(\omega)
. Since
\rho^{R,\epsilon}-R^{-1}\in C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
, we have that
\displaystyle\epsilon\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Delta(R^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon})\text{sgn}^{+}(R^{-1}-\rho^{R,\epsilon})\leq 0
by pg. 64 of
[
22
]
. So we have that
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\rho^{R,\epsilon}-R^{-1})^{-}(\tau)=0
and thus
\rho^{R,\epsilon}\geq 1/R
for all
(t,x)\in[0,\tau(\omega)]\times\mathbb{T}
. This contradicts our assumption.
∎

2.4.
Continuous dependence on the initial condition of the approximate system.

To complete the verification of Hadamard well-posedness for the approximate system, we finally show the following continuous dependence result for the approximate system (
20
).

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
(\rho,q)
and
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})
be two solutions in
C(0,T;\mathcal{X})
to (
20
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
and
(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{q}_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
. Then, for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\mathbb{E}\|(\rho(t),q(t))-(\tilde{\rho}(t),\tilde{q}(t))\|_{C(0,T;\mathcal{X})}\leq C(T,R,\epsilon)\|(\rho_{0},q_{0})-(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{q}_{0})\|_{\mathcal{X}},

where the constant
C(T,R,\epsilon)
depends only on
T
,
R
, and
\epsilon
.

This will be accomplished via a priori estimates, on all spatial derivatives up to the second spatial derivative. Before doing the a priori estimates, we make the following observations, which will be useful for estimating the difference between two solutions to (
20
).

Lemma 2.2
.

For
(\rho,q)
and
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})
in
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
with
\rho,\tilde{\rho}\geq 1/R
and
\max\Big(\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},\|\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\leq M
for some constant
M>0
, the following estimates hold:

(41)

\displaystyle\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(42)

\displaystyle\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(43)

\displaystyle\Big|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

(44)

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)q-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\tilde{q}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(45)

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

(46)

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\rho-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

(47)

\displaystyle\|\rho^{-\alpha}-\tilde{\rho}^{-\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M,\alpha}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\qquad\text{ for }\alpha\geq 1.

Furthermore, for
i,j=0,1,2
:

(48)

\begin{cases}\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{i}q\partial_{x}^{j}q-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{q}\partial_{x}^{j}\tilde{q}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}&\leq C_{R}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),\\
\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{i}\rho\partial_{x}^{i}q-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{\rho}\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{q}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}&\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),\\
\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{i}\rho\partial_{x}^{j}\rho-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{\rho}\partial_{x}^{j}\tilde{\rho}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}&\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).\end{cases}

Proof.

We prove the series of inequalities as follows.

Proof of truncation estimates (
41
)–(
43
).
Note that since
\chi_{R}
is a smooth function satisfying
0\leq\chi_{R}\leq 1
and
\sup_{z\geq 0}|\chi_{R}^{\prime}(z)|\leq C_{R}
for a constant
C_{R}
depending only on
R
, we can estimate:

\displaystyle\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\Big|\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}-\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|\rho^{-1}-\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R}\left\|\frac{\rho-\tilde{\rho}}{\rho\tilde{\rho}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

since
\rho,\tilde{\rho}\geq 1/R
by assumption. Similarly, we estimate that

\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|\leq C_{R}\Big|\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}-\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big|\leq C_{R}\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

By combining these two estimates (
41
) and (
42
), and using the fact that
0\leq\chi_{R}\leq 1
, we also obtain the estimate (
43
) via

|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})|\leq\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|\cdot\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\\
+\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|.

Proof of (
44
) and (
45
).
Next, we prove the fourth inequality (
44
). To show this, note that if both
\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\geq R+1
and
\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\geq R+1
, then the left-hand side of (
44
) is zero and the inequality is trivial. So suppose that at least one of
q
and
\tilde{q}
has
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
norm less than
R+1
, and without loss of generality, let
\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}<R+1
. Then, by (
42
):

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)q

\displaystyle-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\tilde{q}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|\cdot\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}(R+1)\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R}\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

To show the fifth inequality (
45
), we estimate the left-hand side of the fourth inequality as

\Big\|\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)q-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\tilde{q}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq I_{1}+I_{2},

for
I_{1}
and
I_{2}
defined by:

I_{1}:=\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|\cdot\Big\|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)q\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

I_{2}:=\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big\|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)q-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\tilde{q}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

We then immediately obtain the desired inequality (
45
) from the past results (
41
) and (
44
), the bound
0\leq\chi_{R}\leq 1
, and the fact that
\Big\|\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)q\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq R+1
.

Proof of (
46
).
We estimate that

\Big\|\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\rho-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\tilde{\rho}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq I_{1}+I_{2},

where

I_{1}:=\Big|\chi_{R}\Big(\|\rho^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|q\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)-\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\Big|\cdot\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

I_{2}:=\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{\rho}^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\cdot\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

By the assumption
\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
and the estimate (
43
):

I_{1}\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

Since
0\leq\chi_{R}\leq 1
, we immediately have
I_{2}\leq\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}
, which establishes the estimate.

Proof of density estimates (
47
).
We calculate that

\|\rho^{-\alpha}-\tilde{\rho}^{-\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}=\left\|\frac{\rho^{\alpha}-\tilde{\rho}^{\alpha}}{\rho^{\alpha}\tilde{\rho}^{\alpha}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq R^{2\alpha}\|\rho^{\alpha}-\tilde{\rho}^{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M,\alpha}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M,\alpha}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

using the almost sure lower bound on
\rho,\tilde{\rho}\geq 1/R
and the almost sure upper bound
\rho,\tilde{\rho}\leq C_{M}
from the assumption
\max\Big(\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},\|\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\leq M
.

Proof of quadratic estimates (
48
).
To show the last estimate, note that

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{i}q\partial_{x}^{j}q-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{q}\partial_{x}^{j}\tilde{q}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\Big\|\Big(\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)}\partial_{x}^{i}q-\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})}\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{q}\Big)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big\|\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)}\partial_{x}^{j}q\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle+\Big\|\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})}\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{q}\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big\|\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)}\partial_{x}^{j}q-\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})}\partial_{x}^{j}\tilde{q}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|\Big(\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)}q-\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})}\tilde{q}\Big)\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\cdot\Big(\Big\|\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)}q\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\Big\|\sqrt{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})}\tilde{q}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

Here, we use the fifth estimate (
45
) (which still holds in this case since
\sqrt{\chi_{R}}
is also smooth and hence the proof of the estimate (
45
) remains unchanged in this case), and the support properties of
\chi_{R}
and hence
\sqrt{\chi_{R}}
to obtain this estimate. We can similarly obtain the other estimates in (
48
) analogously, using (
45
) and (
47
).
∎

We then use these inequalities to estimate differences of nonlinear terms in the equations.

Lemma 2.3
.

For nonnegative
\rho,\tilde{\rho}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
satisfying the pointwise lower bounds
\rho\geq 1/R
,
\tilde{\rho}\geq 1/R
, and
\max\Big(\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},\|\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\leq M
for some positive constant
M
, we have the following estimates:

\displaystyle\|[q]_{R}-[\tilde{q}]_{R}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{[q]_{R}q}{\rho}-\frac{[\tilde{q}]_{R}\tilde{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\rho^{\gamma}-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma}\Big\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

Proof.

The first inequality is the fifth inequality (
45
) proved in Lemma
2.2
. For the second inequality, we have the following estimates, using (
47
) and (
48
) in Lemma
2.2
and
\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{i}q\partial_{x}^{j}q\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R}
for
i,j=0,1
by the Sobolev embedding
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\subset W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
:

\left\|\frac{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q^{2}}{\rho}-\frac{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}^{2}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

We can similarly obtain the same estimate for the following quantities:

\left\|\frac{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)(\partial_{x}q)^{2}}{\rho}-\frac{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})(\partial_{x}\tilde{q})^{2}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},\qquad\left\|\frac{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q(\partial_{x}q)}{\rho}-\frac{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}(\partial_{x}\tilde{q})}{\tilde{\rho}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

\left\|\frac{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q\partial_{x}^{2}q}{\rho}-\frac{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

where we use
\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q\partial_{x}^{2}q\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R}
by the truncation to estimate the last quantity. It remains to estimate two more quantities. For
i=1,2
:

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\frac{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q\partial_{x}^{i}\rho}{\rho^{2}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{\rho}}{\tilde{\rho}^{2}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|\rho^{-2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{i}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle+\|\rho^{-2}-\tilde{\rho}^{-2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{\rho}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}+\left\|\frac{\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{\rho}}{\tilde{\rho}^{2}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

where we use the assumptions
\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
and
\rho,\tilde{\rho}\geq 1/R
with (
45
) and (
47
). Finally, we estimate:

\displaystyle\Bigg\|\frac{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}}{\rho^{3}}-\frac{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}}{\tilde{\rho}^{3}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|\rho^{-3}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}-(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle+\|\rho^{-3}-\tilde{\rho}^{-3}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}+\left\|\frac{(\partial_{x}^{i}\tilde{\rho})^{2}}{\tilde{\rho}^{3}}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}.

We estimate, using the assumption that
\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
(and similarly with
\tilde{\rho}
) along with Sobolev embedding, that

\|(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}\|_{L^{4}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq C_{R,M},

\|(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}-(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|\partial_{x}(\rho+\tilde{\rho})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

Hence, by (
45
) and (
47
):

\Bigg\|\frac{\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}}{\rho^{3}}-\frac{\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{q}(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}}{\tilde{\rho}^{3}}\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

To prove the last inequality, we estimate it as

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\rho^{\gamma}-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\|(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q}))\rho^{\gamma}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\cdot(\rho^{\gamma}-\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\rho^{\gamma}-\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

where we use (
43
) and the assumption that
1/R\leq\rho\leq C_{M}
combined with the mean value theorem. For the first derivative, we estimate:

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)

\displaystyle\rho^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\rho-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})|\cdot\|\rho^{\gamma-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}\rho\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle+\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\|\rho^{\gamma-1}-\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}\rho\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

We use (
43
), the assumption that
1/R\leq\rho\leq C_{M}
, the mean value theorem, and the assumption that
\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
to conclude that

\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\rho^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\rho-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

Finally, for the second derivative, we estimate two quantities. We first estimate:

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)

\displaystyle\rho^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}^{2}\rho-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{\rho}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})|\cdot\|\rho^{\gamma-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{2}\rho\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle+\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\|\rho^{\gamma-1}-\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{2}\rho\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{2}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

similarly to the first derivative computation. We also estimate:

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)

\displaystyle\rho^{\gamma-2}(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-2}(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})|\cdot\|\rho^{\gamma-2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle+\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\|\rho^{\gamma-2}-\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma-2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}-(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

Here, we estimate using
\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
and
\|\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
, along with Sobolev embedding, that

\|(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|\partial_{x}\rho\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq C_{M},

\|(\partial_{x}\rho)^{2}-(\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho})^{2}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq\|\partial_{x}\rho+\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\cdot\|\partial_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M}\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

This completes the proof of the last inequality.
∎

We also use the estimates in Lemma
2.2
to prove estimates on the difference of the terms arising from the stochastic noise.

Lemma 2.4
.

Suppose that
\rho,\tilde{\rho}\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
with
\rho\geq 1/R
,
\tilde{\rho}\geq 1/R
, and
\max\Big(\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})},\|\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\leq M
for some positive constants
R,M>0
. We then have the following estimates for
i=0,1,2
, for a constant
C_{R,M,\epsilon}
that depends only on
R
,
M
, and
\epsilon
(and independent of
k
):

(49)

\Bigg\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{i}\Big[G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\Big]-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{i}\Big[G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big]\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M,\epsilon}\alpha_{k}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

for any positive integer
k
, where
\alpha_{k}
is defined in (
4
). Hence, for
A_{0}
defined in (
21
), we have that

(50)

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Bigg\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{i}\Big[G^{R,\epsilon}_{k}(\rho,q)\Big]-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{i}\Big[G^{R,\epsilon}_{k}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q}\Big]\Bigg\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq C_{R,M,\epsilon}A_{0}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

Proof.

The second statement (
50
