) follows immediately from the first statement (
49
) using the definition of
A_{0}
in (
21
). We hence prove the first statement (
49
), and we focus on the case of
i=2
, since the other cases of
i=0,1
are easier variations of the same argument. By the Chain Rule:

\partial_{x}^{2}\Big[G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\Big]=\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}(\rho,q)+\langle\nabla_{\rho,q}^{2}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}(\rho,q),\partial_{x}(\rho,q)\rangle.

So it suffices to estimate the following two terms:

(51)

\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})},

(52)

\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\langle\nabla_{\rho,q}^{2}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}(\rho,q),\partial_{x}(\rho,q)\rangle-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\langle\nabla_{\rho,q}^{2}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q}),\partial_{x}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\rangle\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}.

For the term (
51
), we estimate using (
34
), (
45
), and (
46
):

\Big\|\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{2}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial^{2}_{x}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big)\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M,\epsilon}\alpha_{k}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

and we use (
34
), along with the fact that
\|\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})(\rho,q)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M}
by the definition of the truncation and
\|\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
, to conclude that

\displaystyle\Big\|\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{2}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big(\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)

\displaystyle-\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}\alpha_{k}\Big\|\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{2}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,M}\alpha_{k}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

For the term (
52
), we use the quadratic estimates (
48
) and the boundedness of the derivatives of the truncated noise coefficient (
34
) to conclude that:

\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\langle\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}(\rho,q),\partial_{x}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\langle\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q}),\partial_{x}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\rangle\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\\
\leq C_{R,M,\epsilon}\alpha_{k}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

Furthermore, by a Lipschitz estimate on the truncated noise which follows from (
34
), and by the fact that
\Big\|\chi_{R}(\rho,q)|\partial_{x}(\rho,q)|^{2}\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M}
by Sobolev embedding, the definition of the truncation, and
\|\rho\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M
, we conclude that

\Big\|\chi(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\langle\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)-\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q}),\partial_{x}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\rangle\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,M,\epsilon}\alpha_{k}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

∎

Now, we proceed with the a priori estimates for continuous dependence, and we will often use the inequalities in Lemma
2.3
and Lemma
2.4
in these estimates. For these a priori estimates, we consider initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0}),(\tilde{\rho}_{0},\tilde{q}_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
(see (
37
)), and we solve the approximate system (
20
) with this initial data to obtain corresponding unique solutions
(\rho,q)
and
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})
that are both in
C(0,T;\mathcal{X})
almost surely. We want to estimate difference between these two solutions
(\rho-\tilde{\rho},q-\tilde{q})
in terms of the difference of the initial data. These a priori estimates will be the content of the proof of Proposition
2.3
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3
.

The goal of the a priori estimates is to obtain a Gronwall-type inequality for the quantity

\mathbb{E}\Big(\|\rho(t)-\tilde{\rho}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q(t)-\tilde{q}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

We recall from Lemma
2.1
and Proposition
2.2
that the solutions
(\rho,q)
and
(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})
satisfy the following almost sure bounds:

\|\rho\|_{C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{R,T},\quad\|\tilde{\rho}\|_{C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{R,T},\quad\rho(t,\cdot)\geq 1/R,\quad\tilde{\rho}(t,\cdot)\geq 1/R.

This will allow us to apply Lemma
2.3
and Lemma
2.4
, where these types of bounds are required as assumptions in order to derive the estimates.

We obtain the following equations for the difference
(\rho-\tilde{\rho},q-\tilde{q})
:

(53)

\partial_{t}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})+\partial_{x}([q]_{R}-[\tilde{q}]_{R})=\epsilon\Delta(\rho-\tilde{\rho}),

(54)

\partial_{t}(q-\tilde{q})+\partial_{x}\left(\frac{[q]_{R}q}{\rho}-\frac{[\tilde{q}]_{R}\tilde{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}(\kappa\rho^{\gamma})-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}(\kappa\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma})\\
=\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big)dW-\alpha(q-\tilde{q})+\epsilon\Delta(q-\tilde{q}).

We differentiate each equation (
53
) and (
54
) by
\partial_{x}^{j}
for
j=0,1,2
, and then use the estimates in Lemma
2.3
to prove the continuous dependence result in Proposition
2.3
via Gronwall’s inequality. From the first equation (
53
), we obtain:

(55)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{j}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})(t)|^{2}dx+\epsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j+1}_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{j}(\rho_{0}-\tilde{\rho}_{0})|^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}^{j}\Big([q]_{R}-[\tilde{q}]_{R}\Big)\partial_{x}^{j+1}(\rho-\tilde{\rho}).

Using Itö’s formula in (
54
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{j}(q-\tilde{q})(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j}_{x}(q-\tilde{q})|^{2}+\epsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j+1}_{x}(q-\tilde{q})|^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial^{j}_{x}\left(\frac{[q]_{R}q}{\rho}-\frac{[\tilde{q}]_{R}\tilde{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)\partial^{j+1}_{x}(q-\tilde{q})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{j}(\kappa\rho^{\gamma})-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{j}(\kappa\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma})\Big)\partial_{x}^{j+1}(q-\tilde{q})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}^{j}\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big)\partial_{x}^{j}(q-\tilde{q})dW

(56)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}^{j}\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big)\right)^{2}.

By using Lemma
2.3
, we estimate for (
55
) that

(57)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}^{j}\Big([q]_{R}-[\tilde{q}]_{R}\Big)\partial^{j+1}_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j+1}_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})|^{2}+C_{\epsilon,R,T}\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|[q]_{R}-[\tilde{q}]_{R}\Big\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j+1}_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})|^{2}+C_{\epsilon,R,T}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big),

where by using Cauchy’s inequality (with
\epsilon
), we can absorb the term
\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j+1}_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})|^{2}
into the dissipation term on the left-hand side of (
55
). For (
2.4
), we note that by taking
expectation
of both sides, the stochastic integral has expectation zero and hence vanishes from the computation. For the first two nonlinear difference terms on the right-hand side of (
2.4
), we use Cauchy with epsilon along with the estimates in Lemma
2.3
to estimate:

(58)

\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{x}^{j}\left(\frac{[q]_{R}q}{\rho}-\frac{[\tilde{q}]_{R}\tilde{q}}{\tilde{\rho}}\right)\partial^{j+1}_{x}(q-\tilde{q})+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}^{j}(\kappa\rho^{\gamma})-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\partial_{x}^{j}(\kappa\tilde{\rho}^{\gamma})\Big)\partial^{j+1}_{x}(q-\tilde{q})\right|\\
\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j+1}_{x}(q-\tilde{q})|^{2}+C_{\epsilon,R,T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big).

We can also use the estimate in Lemma
2.4
to estimate the quadratic variation term:

(59)

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big|\partial^{j}_{x}\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\tilde{\rho},\tilde{q})\Big)\Big|^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon,R,T}A_{0}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|\rho-\tilde{\rho}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\|q-\tilde{q}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\Big)ds.

By taking expectations in (
53
) and (
54
) and adding over
j=0,1,2
, and then applying the estimates (
57
), (
58
), and (
59
), we obtain the desired estimate by absorbing terms into the dissipation term on the left-hand side:

(60)

\mathbb{E}\Big(\|(\rho-\tilde{\rho})(t)\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|(q-\tilde{q})(t)\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|\partial_{x}(\rho-\tilde{\rho})(s)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\|\partial_{x}(q-\tilde{q})(s)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\Big)\\
\leq\Big(\|\rho_{0}-\tilde{\rho}_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\|q_{0}-\tilde{q}_{0}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\Big)+C_{\epsilon,R,T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|(\rho-\tilde{\rho})(s)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\|(q-\tilde{q})(s)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)ds.

The result then follows from an application of Gronwall’s inequality.
∎

2.5.
Feller semigroup for the approximate system.

Since the approximate system (
20
) has a notion of Hadamard well-posedness, we claim that we can define an associated
Feller semigroup

\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
to the evolution of the approximate system. Recall the definition of the phase space
\mathcal{X}
from (
37
):

\mathcal{X}:=\left\{(\rho,q)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{T}):\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(x)dx=1\text{ and }\rho\geq\frac{1}{R}\right\}.

Consider any deterministic state
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
. Note that by the global existence and uniqueness result in Proposition
2.3
for the approximate system, we can define the evolution

\Big(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t;(\rho_{0},q_{0})),q^{R,\epsilon}(t;(\rho_{0},q_{0}))\Big)\in\mathcal{X}\qquad\text{ for }t\geq 0,

which we define to be the (random) solution at time
t
to the approximate system (
20
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})
.

Let
C_{b}(\mathcal{X})
denote the space of bounded continuous functions
\varphi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then, for each time
t\geq 0
, we can define an operator
\mathcal{P}_{t}
acting on functions in
C_{b}(\mathcal{X})
, defined by

(61)

(\mathcal{P}_{t}\varphi)(\rho_{0},q_{0})=\mathbb{E}\Big[\varphi\Big(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t;(\rho_{0},q_{0})),q^{R,\epsilon}(t;(\rho_{0},q_{0}))\Big)\Big].

We claim that
\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a Feller semigroup, namely that it has the properties listed in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.4
.

The collection
\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a Feller semigroup in the sense that
\mathcal{P}_{t}:C_{b}(\mathcal{X})\to C_{b}(\mathcal{X})
for each
t\geq 0
, and for all
s,t\geq 0
:

\mathcal{P}_{0}\varphi=\varphi\ \ \text{ and }\ \ \mathcal{P}_{s}(\mathcal{P}_{t}\varphi)=\mathcal{P}_{s+t}\varphi,\qquad\text{ for all }\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathcal{X}),

Proof.

It is immediate that
\mathcal{P}_{0}=\text{Id}
, and the semigroup property
\mathcal{P}_{s}\circ\mathcal{P}_{t}=\mathcal{P}_{s+t}
follows from the uniqueness result in Proposition
2.1
. The fact that
\mathcal{P}_{t}
is a bounded continous operator on
C_{b}(\mathcal{X})
, namely that
\mathcal{P}_{t}\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathcal{X})
for all
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathcal{X})
, is a direct consequence of the continuous dependence property in Proposition
2.3
.
∎

3.
Results on uniform invariant regions

Now that we have a Feller semigroup
\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
for the approximate system (
20
), we will use a time-averaging procedure to obtain an invariant measure (and hence statistically stationary solution) on
\mathcal{X}
for the approximate system. To carry out this time averaging procedure, we need uniform estimates on the approximate system in time for a general (stochastic) solution
(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t),q^{R,\epsilon}(t))
to the initial value problem in (
20
). This will be the content of the current section and also Section
4
.

In this section, we will deduce uniform in time
L^{\infty}
bounds on the solution
(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t),q^{R,\epsilon}(t))
to the initial value problem (
20
), using the structure of
invariant regions
for the isentropic Euler equations. In particular, we note that the structure of the truncations we use in the approximate system (
20
) preserve the structure of invariant regions to the damped compressible Euler equations, which we can use to obtain uniform
L^{\infty}
bounds independently of
T
and
R
.

It is well-known that the (undamped) deterministic compressible isentropic Euler equations in one spatial dimension with artificial viscosity

(62)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\partial_{x}q=\epsilon\Delta\rho,\\
\partial_{t}q+\partial_{x}\left(\frac{q^{2}}{\rho}\right)+\partial_{x}(\kappa\rho^{\gamma})=\epsilon\Delta q.\end{cases}

possess the Riemann invariants

z=u-\rho^{\theta},\qquad w=u+\rho^{\theta},

and an associated invariant region

\Lambda_{\kappa}=\{(\rho,u)\in(0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}:-\kappa\leq z\leq w\leq\kappa\}.

This region is invariant in the sense that any classical solution to (
62
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
for some
\kappa>0
will remain within
\Lambda_{\kappa}
for all times
t\geq 0
. This is the result of
[
14
]
(see also
[
20
]
), which essentially involves a change of variables via the Riemann invariants with a maximum/minimum principle type argument.

In this section, we consider the following deterministic
truncated
system with damping, which differs from the classical scenario in (
62
) and gives the approximate problem we are considering in (
20
) when
\delta=0
:

(63)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}q=\epsilon\Delta\rho,\\
\partial_{t}q+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}\left(\frac{q^{2}}{\rho}+\kappa\rho^{\gamma}\right)=-\alpha q+\epsilon\Delta q.\end{cases}

We claim that this system has the same invariant region
\Lambda_{\kappa}
(for arbitrary
\kappa>0
)
independently of the truncation parameter
R
. Moreover, the fact that the truncated equations have the same invariant region will then give us the following uniform bound on the classical solutions
(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t),q^{R,\epsilon}(t))
to the initial value problem for initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
, that are constructed in Proposition
2.1
.

Proposition 3.1
.

For initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\mathcal{X}
, the classical solution
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
to (
20
) in Proposition
2.1
satisfies the following uniform bounds:

\left\|\left(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon},\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)\right\|_{C(0,T;C(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon}\quad\text{ almost surely. }

for a deterministic constant
C_{\epsilon}
that is independent of
T
and
R
but dependent on
\epsilon
.

This proposition will be a consequence of the following result on invariant regions for the deterministic damped truncated system in (
63
). (Note that even though the approximate system is stochastic, due to the regularization of the noise in Section
2.2
at the
\epsilon
level, the
\epsilon
-regularized noise is compactly supported and hence also respects the invariant region structure of the damped Euler equations, see Proposition
3.3
.)

Proposition 3.2
.

The region

(64)

\Lambda_{\kappa}=\{(\rho,q)\in(0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}:-\kappa\leq z\leq w\leq\kappa\},\qquad\text{ for }z=\frac{q}{\rho}-\rho^{\theta}\text{ and }w=\frac{q}{\rho}+\rho^{\theta}

is an invariant region for (
63
) in the sense that for any spatially smooth initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})
such that
(\rho_{0}(x),q_{0}(x))\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
for all
x\in\mathbb{T}
, the unique global smooth solution with initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})
has
(\rho(t,x),q(t,x))\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
for all
t\geq 0
and
x\in\mathbb{T}
.

The proof of this proposition is essentially a minimum/maximum principle type argument, but with a nonlinear transformation given by the Riemann invariants. As is customary in some minimum and maximum principle arguments, to have the strict inequalities required for such arguments, we use a perturbation by a parameter
\delta>0
to help with the proof of the result, and then pass to the limit as
\delta\to 0
to obtain a result for the original set of equations. Namely, we consider

(65)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}q=-\delta\rho+\epsilon\Delta\rho,\\
\partial_{t}q+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}\Big(\frac{q^{2}}{\rho}+\kappa\rho^{\gamma}\Big)=-\alpha q+\epsilon\Delta q.\end{cases}

We hence first show the following invariant region result for the perturbed
\delta
-system (
65
) and then pass to the limit as
\delta\to 0
to prove Proposition
3.2
.
This is done in the spirit of the invariant region results in
[
14
]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

The region
\Lambda_{\kappa}
is an invariant region for (
65
), whenever
0<\delta<\alpha
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

We rewrite the system (
65
) as

(66)

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{U}+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}=\epsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{U}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{U})\text{ on }{\mathbb{R}}^{+}\times\mathbb{T},\qquad\boldsymbol{U}(0)=\boldsymbol{U}_{0}:=\begin{pmatrix}\rho_{0}\\
q_{0}\\
\end{pmatrix},

where

\boldsymbol{U}:=\begin{pmatrix}\rho\\
q\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{F}(\boldsymbol{U})=\begin{pmatrix}q\\
\frac{q^{2}}{\rho}+\kappa\rho^{\gamma}\end{pmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U})=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\frac{q^{2}}{\rho^{2}}+\kappa\gamma\rho^{\gamma-1}&\frac{2q}{\rho}\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\boldsymbol{G}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{U}):=\begin{pmatrix}-\delta\rho\\
-\alpha q\\
\end{pmatrix}.

In terms of the state variables
(\rho,q)
, we can rewrite the Riemann invariants as

(67)

z=\frac{q}{\rho}-\rho^{\theta},\qquad w=\frac{q}{\rho}+\rho^{\theta}.

Hence, we compute that

\nabla_{\rho,q}z=\left(-\frac{q}{\rho^{2}}-\theta\rho^{\theta-1},\frac{1}{\rho}\right),\qquad\nabla_{\rho,q}w=\left(-\frac{q}{\rho^{2}}+\theta\rho^{\theta-1},\frac{1}{\rho}\right)

and we observe that
\nabla_{\rho,q}z
and
\nabla_{\rho,q}w
are left eigenvectors of the matrix
\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U})
with eigenvalues
\displaystyle\lambda_{z}=\frac{q}{\rho}-\theta\rho^{\theta}
and
\displaystyle\lambda_{w}=\frac{q}{\rho}+\theta\rho^{\theta}
respectively.

We now prove the desired claim about invariant regions by using the truncated system written in quasilinear form (
66
). Consider initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
, defined in (
64
), and let
\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}=(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))
be the solution to (
66
). Suppose that there exists
t_{0}>0
such that

z(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=-\kappa\ \ \text{ or }\ \ w(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=\kappa,\quad\text{ for some $x_{0}\in\mathbb{T}$},

and

(68)

-\kappa\leq z(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\leq w(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\leq\kappa,\qquad\text{ for all }0\leq t<t_{0}\text{ and }x\in\mathbb{T}.

We claim that

\begin{cases}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big(z(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\Big)\Big|_{(t,x)=(t_{0},x_{0})}>0\text{ in the case where }z(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=-\kappa,\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big(w(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\Big)\Big|_{(t,x)=(t_{0},x_{0})}<0\text{ in the case where $w(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=\kappa$}.\end{cases}

To prove this, we consider the case where

(69)

w(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=\kappa

with the objective of showing that
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}w\Big(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0})\Big)<0
, as the case of showing the claim for
z
follows analogously. We use (
66
) and the Chain Rule to compute that

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})=\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\Big(\epsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})-\chi_{R}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\Big).

We will perform a sign analysis on the various terms on the right-hand side, evaluated at the point
(t_{0},x_{0})
, in order to show the desired result.

Term 1.
It can be shown (see Section 4 in
[
20
]
) that
w
is
quasiconvex
, meaning that

\nabla_{\rho,q}w\cdot\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}|_{(t,x)}=0\text{ at some point $(t,x)$}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\langle(\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}w)\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\rangle|_{(t,x)}\geq 0.

Recall that there is a local maximum in space of
w(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))
at
(t_{0},x_{0})
by assumption in (
68
) and (
69
). So by considering the quadratic term in the Taylor expansion in the
x
variable around
x_{0}
(for fixed
t_{0}
):

\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}+\langle(\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}w)\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\rangle|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}\leq 0.

So the quasiconvexity of
w
implies that

\epsilon\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\cdot\partial_{x}^{2}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}=\epsilon\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}\leq 0.

Term 2.
We compute that

\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\boldsymbol{G}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})=-(\alpha-\delta)\frac{q_{\delta}}{\rho_{\delta}}-\delta\theta\rho^{\theta}_{\delta}.

Since
w(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=\kappa
for
w=\frac{q_{\delta}}{\rho_{\delta}}+\rho_{\delta}^{\theta}
and
-\kappa\leq z(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\leq w(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\leq\kappa
for all
0\leq t<t_{0}
by assumption in (
68
) and (
69
), we conclude by the geometry of the invariant region
\Lambda_{\kappa}
that
0<\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0})\leq\kappa^{1/\theta}
and
q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0})\geq 0
by solving the inequalities for
z
and
w
, using (
67
).

Next, note that if
(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})
satisfies (
65
), then
(\bar{\rho}_{\delta},\bar{q}_{\delta})=e^{\delta t}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})
satisfies

\partial_{t}\bar{\rho}_{\delta}+\chi_{R}(\bar{\rho}_{\delta}e^{-\delta t},\bar{q}_{\delta}e^{-\delta t})\partial_{x}\bar{q}_{\delta}=\epsilon\Delta\bar{\rho}_{\delta}.

An easy generalization of the result in Proposition
2.2
via a minimum principle argument gives us
\frac{1}{R}\leq\bar{\rho}_{\delta}(t,x)
a.s. almost surely for all
t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{T}
. Hence we obtain:

(70)

\displaystyle 0<\frac{e^{-\delta t}}{R}\leq\rho_{\delta}(t,x),\qquad\text{almost surely for all }t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{T},

This gives us that
\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0})
cannot be zero, i.e. the approximate system (
65
) does not have vacuum.

Hence, for
\delta>0
sufficiently small (namely
0<\delta<\alpha
), we have that

\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\boldsymbol{G}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\Big|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}<0.

Note that this is where we needed the extra
\delta
approximation, to get this derivative to be strictly negative (otherwise, if
\delta=0
, it could be zero at
\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0})=\kappa^{1/\theta}
and
q_{\delta}=0
).

Term 3.
Since
\nabla_{\rho,q}w
is a left eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})
with eigenvector
\displaystyle\lambda_{w}=\frac{q_{\delta}}{\rho_{\delta}}+\theta\rho_{\delta}^{\theta}
, we compute that

-\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}=-\lambda_{w}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\chi_{R}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}.

At
(t_{0},x_{0})
,
w(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x))
has a local maximum in space by the assumptions (
68
) and (
69
). So by the Chain Rule,
\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\Big|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}=0
. Hence,

-\chi_{R}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\nabla_{\rho,q}w(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\Big|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}=0.

This establishes the desired result that
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big(w(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\Big)\Big|_{(t,x)=(t_{0},x_{0})}<0
.

Finally, we make a few comments about the other case in which
z(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=-\kappa
, and the assumption (
68
) holds. In this case, we would want to show that

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big(z(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\Big)\Big|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}>0.

By (
66
) and the Chain Rule:

(71)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}z(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})=\nabla_{\rho,q}z(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\Big(\epsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}+G_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})-\chi_{R}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\Big)

In this case, we can estimate the terms on the right-hand side similarly to the case of
w
above. For the first term on the right-hand side,
-z
is quasiconvex (see Section 4 of
[
20
]
), so therefore,

\epsilon\nabla_{\rho,q}z(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\Delta\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}\geq 0.

For the second term, we compute that for
0<\delta<\alpha
:

\nabla_{\rho,q}z(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\boldsymbol{G}_{\delta}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})=-(\alpha-\delta)\frac{q_{\delta}}{\rho_{\delta}}+\delta\theta\rho_{\delta}^{\theta}>0,

since
z(\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}),q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0}))=-\kappa
and the assumption (
68
) together imply that
0<\rho_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0})\leq\kappa^{1/\theta}
and
q_{\delta}(t_{0},x_{0})\leq 0
, from the definition of the Riemann invariants. Finally, in the same way as for Term 3 of the computation for
w
(namely, using the fact that
\nabla_{\rho,q}z
is a left eigenvector of
\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})
), we have that
\nabla_{\rho,q}z(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\Big|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}=0
, and hence:

-\chi_{R}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\nabla_{\rho,q}z(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\delta}\Big|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}=0.

Thus, using (
71
), this completes the proof of the fact that
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}z(\rho_{\delta}(t,x),q_{\delta}(t,x))\Big|_{(t_{0},x_{0})}>0
.
∎

Our next goal is to extend this result in Proposition
3.1
on invariant regions to the equations (
65
) with
\delta>0
to the case of
\delta=0
, by taking a limit as
\delta\to 0
. This will hence prove the invariant region result in Proposition
3.2
for
\delta=0
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

Let
(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})
be the solution to (
65
) for a perturbation parameter
\delta>0
and some initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
, and let
(\rho,u)
be the solution to (
63
) for the same initial data. The proposition will be established if we show that

(72)

\|\rho_{\delta}-\rho\|_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}+\|u_{\delta}-u\|_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}\to 0,\qquad\text{ almost surely.}

The result thus follows when we combine this with the fact that
(\rho_{\delta},u_{\delta})\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
for all
t\geq 0
and for all
0<\delta<\alpha
almost surely.

Subtracting the equations for
(\rho,q)
and
(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})
in (
63
) and (
65
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(\rho-\rho_{\delta})+\partial_{x}([q]_{R}-[q_{\delta}]_{R})=\epsilon\Delta(\rho-\rho_{\delta})-\delta\rho_{\delta},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}(q-q_{\delta})+\partial_{x}\left(\frac{[q]_{R}q}{\rho}-\frac{[q_{\delta}]_{R}q_{\delta}}{\rho_{\delta}}\right)+\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\partial_{x}(\kappa\rho^{\gamma})-\chi_{R}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\partial_{x}(\kappa\rho_{\delta}^{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\Big(\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)-\chi_{R}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\Big)dW-\alpha(q-q_{\delta})+\epsilon\Delta(q-q_{\delta}).

Note that these difference equations are the same as those for the continuous dependence proof in Proposition
2.3
, see (
53
) and (
54
), with an extra
\delta\rho_{\delta}
term in the first equation.
Moreover, observe due to the lower bounds on the density
\rho_{\delta}
given in (
70
)
we get analogues of the estimates in Lemma
2.2
.

So as in the proof of Proposition
2.3
, we can obtain the following analogue of inequality (
60
), where we account for the extra
\delta\rho_{\delta}
term and note that
\rho
and
\rho_{\delta}
have the same initial data:

(73)

\Big(\|\rho-\rho_{\delta}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|(q-q_{\delta})(t)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\Big)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|\partial_{x}(\rho-\rho_{\delta})(s)\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\partial_{x}(q-q_{\delta})(s)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\Big)ds\\
\leq\delta C(\epsilon)\mathbb{E}\Big(\|\rho_{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}^{2}\Big)+C_{R,\epsilon,T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\|(\rho-\rho_{\delta})(s)\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\|(q-q_{\delta})(s)\|^{2}_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)ds.

Here, we used the estimate for
j=0,1,2
that

\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\delta\partial_{x}^{j}\rho_{\delta}\partial_{x}^{j}(\rho-\rho_{\delta})\right|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{j}(\rho-\rho_{\delta})|^{2}+\delta C(\epsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j}_{x}\rho_{\delta}|^{2},

where the term
\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{4}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial^{j}_{x}(\rho-\rho_{\delta})|^{2}
can be absorbed into the dissipation term on the left-hand side of the estimate, as in the proof of Lemma
2.3
.

Identically to the proof of Lemma
2.1
we can show that
\|\rho_{\delta}\|_{C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{R,T}
almost surely, for a constant
C_{R,T}
that is independent of
\delta
. Therefore, using this in (
73
), we obtain by Gronwall’s inequality that

\|\rho-\rho_{\delta}\|_{C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}+\|q-q_{\delta}\|_{C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\to 0,

so since
(\rho_{\delta},q_{\delta})\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
, we conclude by the continuous embedding
C(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\subset C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
that
(\rho,q)\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
also by passing to the limit as
\delta\to 0
.
∎

Now that we have shown that the deterministic dynamics of the approximate system (
20
), represented by (
63
), have an invariant region of
\Lambda_{\kappa}
whenever the initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\Lambda_{\kappa}
, we show a corresponding invariant region result for the stochastic problem.

Proposition 3.3
.

The region
\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
is an invariant region for the stochastic equation:

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho=0,\\
\partial_{t}q=\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)dW.\end{cases}

Namely, given (potentially random) initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})
which is in
\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
, the solution
(\rho(t),q(t))
exists and is in
\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
for all
t\geq 0
almost surely. (See Section
2.2
and (
33
) for the construction of
\kappa_{\epsilon}
).

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the compact support assumption on the noise coefficient
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)
in (
33
), where we construct the regularized and truncated noise coefficient
\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)
so that it has support in
\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
.
∎

Since both the deterministic dynamics (Proposition
3.2
) and the stochastic dynamics (Proposition
3.3
) of the approximate system (
20
) have an invariant region, the coupled dynamics of the full stochastic damped compressible Euler equations give rise to an invariant region too. This allows us to prove Proposition
3.1
on uniform
L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
bounds on
(\rho,q)
as follows.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

We split the problem (
20
) into its deterministic and stochastic components (see e.g. the splitting scheme used in
[
1
]
or
[
3
]
). By combining Proposition
3.2
and Proposition
3.3
, we conclude that the splitting scheme and thus the system (
20
) has an invariant region of
\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
. Namely, given initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\in\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
, the stochastic solution to the approximate system (
20
) is in
\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
for all
t\geq 0
, almost surely. Therefore, since
\rho
and
u
are bounded for all
(\rho,u)\in\Lambda_{\kappa_{\epsilon}}
, we conclude that
\rho
and
q:=\rho u
are bounded almost surely, namely:

\|(\rho,u,q)\|_{C([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}.

∎

4.
Uniform-in-time bounds for the approximate system

In this section, we will obtain additional higher order uniform bounds on the fluid density and the fluid velocity in time, in preparation for the time averaging procedure, see (
84
), that will give the existence of an invariant measure to (
20
). We will consider the approximate solution (
20
) with initial data

(\rho_{0},u_{0})=(1,0),

and derive bounds on the resulting solution
(\rho,u)
that are sublinear in time at the expense of their dependence on the approximating and regularizing parameters
R
and
\epsilon
.

In Propositions
4.1
and
4.2
, we will obtain
H^{3}(\mathbb{T})
bounds for the fluid density and the momentum, as a result of the truncation and additional regularization, which will let us establish tightness of the time-averaged laws in Proposition
5.1
via standard compactness arguments. We note that the choice of
H^{3}(\mathbb{T})
is because of the fact that
H^{3}(\mathbb{T})
compactly embeds into
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
, which is the space for the path space
\mathcal{X}
defined in (
37
).

4.1.
Bounds on the density.

We first start by showing uniform bounds for the density.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
be the unique solution in
C(0,T;\mathcal{X})
to the approximate system (
20
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})=(1,0)
. Then, for all
T\geq 0
:

\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{C(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}dt\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\quad\text{ almost surely},

where the constant
C_{R,\epsilon}
, depending on
R,\epsilon
, is
deterministic
and is
independent of the time
T>0
.

Proof.

By integrating the approximate continuity equation in (
20
) over the spatial domain
\mathbb{T}
, we obtain conservation of mass:

(74)

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,\cdot)dx=1,\quad\text{ almost surely, for all }t\geq 0.

We use this (almost sure) uniform
L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
bound on
\rho^{R,\epsilon}
to bootstrap the uniform-in-time bounds to higher regularity via maximal regularity. Namely, we have, for some constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{-2}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle+\|\Delta\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{-2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C\Big(\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(T,\cdot)\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\partial_{x}[q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{-2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(T,\cdot)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T})}+\|[q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}))}\Big)\leq C_{R},

by the Sobolev embedding
L^{1}(\mathbb{T})\subset H^{-1}(\mathbb{T})
and the definition of the truncation (
18
).
Therefore, for a deterministic constant
c_{0}
that is independent of
T
:

(75)

\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq c_{0}\quad\text{ almost surely.}

Using this uniform bound, we deduce that in every interval
[N/2,(N+1)/2]
for nonnegative integers
N
, there exists a corresponding random
\tau_{0,N}(\omega)\in[N/2,(N+1)/2]
depending on the random outcome in the probability space
\omega\in\Omega
, such that

\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(\omega,\tau_{0,N},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 2c_{0}.

and we note that by maximal regularity (and for a fixed
\omega
in a measurable set of probability one):

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{0,N},\tau_{0,N}+1;H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle+\|\Delta\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{0,N},\tau_{0,N}+1;H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(\tau_{0,N},\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\partial_{x}[q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{0,N},\tau_{0,N}+1;H^{-1}(\mathbb{T}))}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\Big(c_{0}+\|[q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{0,N},\tau_{0,N}+1;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\Big)\leq C(1+2c_{0}).

Note that for (almost every) fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, the corresponding
\{\tau_{0,N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
is a monotonically increasing sequence of times for which
|\tau_{0,N+1}-\tau_{0,N}|\leq 1
. Thus, every interval
[T,T+1]
for arbitrary
T
can be fully covered by five such intervals
[\tau_{0,N},\tau_{0,N}+1]
and hence:

(76)

\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq 5C(1+2c_{0}+R):=c_{1}\quad\text{ almost surely},

for a deterministic constant
c_{1}
that is independent of
T
.

We can then iterate this procedure to bootstrap uniform bounds for higher regularity. We can construct
\tau_{1,N}(\omega)\in[N/2,(N+1)/2]
for each nonnegative integer
N
and outcome
\omega
as before, such that

(77)

\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(\omega,\tau_{1,N},\cdot)\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 2c_{1},

and by maximal regularity, as before:

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{1,N},\tau_{1,N}+1,L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle+\|\Delta\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{1,N},\tau_{1,N}+1;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\Big(\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(\tau_{1,N},\cdot)\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}+\|\partial_{x}([q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R})\|_{L^{2}(\tau_{1,N},\tau_{1,N}+1;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\Big(1+c_{1}\Big),

by (
75
) and (
76
).
A similar covering argument shows that for a deterministic constant
c_{2}
independent of
T>0
,

(78)

\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq c_{2}\quad\text{ almost surely.}

Given the definition of the truncation we have, for any
T>0
, that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}([q^{R,\epsilon}]_{R})\|^{2}_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R}.

By the Sobolev embedding
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\subset W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb{T})
, we can iterate the above procedure once more to obtain a uniform bound for a deterministic constant
c_{3}
that is independent of
T
:

(79)

\|\partial_{t}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}+\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(T,T+1;H^{3}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq c_{3}\quad\text{ almost surely}.

Then, (
79
) immediately implies that

\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}dt\leq C,\qquad\text{ almost surely}

for a deterministic constant
C
that is independent of
T
.

Furthermore, by construction, for every
t\geq 0
, there exists some
\tau_{N}\leq t
such that
t-\tau_{N}\leq 1
. Hence, by (
77
) and (
79
), there exists a uniform in time constant
C
, dependent on
R
and
\epsilon
, such that the following inequality follows from Fundamental Theorem of Calculus:

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C,\quad\text{ almost surely.}

∎

4.2.
Uniform bounds on the fluid velocity.

Finally, we show a uniform bound on the fluid velocity.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let, for
T>0
,
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
be the unique solution to (
20
) for initial data
(\rho_{0},u_{0})=(1,0)
. Then, there exists a constant
C
that is independent of
T>0
such that

\frac{1}{T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{{T}}\|q^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}dt\leq C.

Proof.

Zeroth order derivative estimate on the momentum.
We consider the momentum equation for
q^{R,\epsilon}=\rho^{R,\epsilon}u^{R,\epsilon}
in (
20
):

\partial_{t}q^{R,\epsilon}+\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}\left(\frac{(q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)+\kappa\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}((\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma})\\
=\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\Phi^{R,\epsilon}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})dW-\alpha q^{R,\epsilon}+\epsilon\Delta q^{R,\epsilon}.

By applying Itô’s formula with the functional
q^{R,\epsilon}\to\frac{1}{2}\|q^{R,\epsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}
, we obtain for any
t\in[0,T]
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|q^{R,\epsilon}(t)|^{2}dx+\alpha\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}dxdt+\epsilon\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}\left(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}\right)\frac{(q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}(\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon})dxdt+{\kappa\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}\left(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}\right)(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma}(\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon})dxdt}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\chi(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})|^{2}|\boldsymbol{G}^{R,\epsilon}(x,\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})|^{2}dxdt+\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}}q^{R,\epsilon}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})dW_{k},

where
W_{k}=W\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
. Recall that
(\rho_{0},q_{0})=(1,0)
, so that
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}|q(0)|^{2}dx=0
.
Now we take
t=T
and apply expectation on both sides and estimate the terms on the right-hand side.
First, observe that due to the uniform bounds on
q^{R,\epsilon},\rho^{R,\epsilon}
and
\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}
obtained in Proposition
3.1
, we can immediately deduce by Young’s inequality that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}\left(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}\right)\left(\kappa\gamma(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma}+\frac{(q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)(\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon})dxdt\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma}+\frac{(q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right||\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+C_{\epsilon}T,

Similarly, using
|\boldsymbol{G}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})|^{2}\leq A_{0}(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}
from (
36
) on the final term, we thus conclude that

(80)

\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt\leq C_{\epsilon}T,

where
C_{\epsilon}
is
independent of both
R
and
T
.

First order derivative estimate on the momentum.
Next, we estimate the higher derivatives. We differentiate by
x
and apply Ito formula with
\frac{1}{2}\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}
. By integrating by parts appropriately, we obtain:

(81)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}&|\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}(T)|^{2}dx+\alpha\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+\epsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt\\
&=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}({\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}})\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}dxdt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}({\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}})\partial_{x}\left(\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)q^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}dxdt\\
&+\kappa\gamma\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}\left(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}\right)(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}dxdt\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}|\nabla_{\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})|^{2}dxdt\\
&+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\nabla_{\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}dW_{k},\end{split}

where
W_{k}=W\boldsymbol{e}_{k}
. We again take expectation on both sides and find bounds for the terms on the right hand side.
Thanks to the definition of
\chi_{R}(\rho,q^{R,\epsilon})
and Proposition
3.1
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}({\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}})\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}dxdt\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+C_{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big[\chi_{R}({\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}})\Big]^{2}|\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}\left(\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dx.

Similarly, for the second term, we additionally use Proposition
4.1
and the definition of the truncation
\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})=\chi_{R}\Big(\|(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{-1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\chi_{R}\Big(\|q^{R,\epsilon}\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)
in (
18
), to obtain

\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}q^{R,\epsilon}\chi_{R}({\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}})\partial_{x}\left(\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}\right|\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dx.

Again from the uniform in time bound of
\rho^{R,\epsilon}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})
for all time in Proposition
4.1
, and the embedding
H^{1}(\mathbb{T})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\kappa\gamma\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+C_{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2(\gamma-1)}|\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}T+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt.

Finally, by (
36
), (
80
), Proposition
4.1
, and the computation that
\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}=\rho\nabla_{\rho,q}g_{k}^{R,\epsilon}+(1,0)g_{k}^{R,\epsilon}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})

\displaystyle\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq 2A_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(1+(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2})\Big((\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}+(\partial_{x}(q^{R,\epsilon})^{2})\Big)dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T.

So we conclude that

(82)

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T.

Second order derivative estimate on the momentum.
Finally, we differentiate the momentum equation twice and test with
\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}
to obtain:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}(T)|^{2}dx+\alpha\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left[(\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon})\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}+2(\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon})\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}\left(\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)\right]\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}dxdt

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left[q^{R,\epsilon}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)\right]\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}dxdt

\displaystyle+\kappa\gamma\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}\left(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}\right)\Big((\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}^{2}\rho^{R,\epsilon}+(\gamma-1)(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma-2}(\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}\Big)\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}dxdt

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}|\langle\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}G^{R,\epsilon}_{k}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}),\partial_{x}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\rangle|^{2}dxdt.

In the same way as for the first order derivative estimate, we can estimate the first two terms in absolute values on the right hand side using Proposition
4.1
and (
80
) as

\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt.

For the third term, the definition of
\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
in (
18
) combined with Sobolev embedding, gives us:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|q^{R,\epsilon}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}\right|dxdt

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|q^{R,\epsilon}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\left((\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{-1}\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}-2\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon}\frac{\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}}{(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}}-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}}{(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}}-2\frac{(\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}}{(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{3}}\right)q^{R,\epsilon}\right)\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}\right|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(|\partial_{x}^{2}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+|\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}+|\partial^{2}_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}+|\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{4}\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt.

Recall from Proposition
4.1
that

\sup_{t\geq 0}\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,\cdot)\|_{C(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{R,\epsilon},\qquad\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{3}(\mathbb{T}))}^{2}\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T.

Hence due to the following interpolation inequality

(83)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{4}(\mathbb{T})}^{4}\leq\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{2}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2},

and the uniform bounds
\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{C(0,T;C(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{R,\epsilon}
and
\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}^{2}\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T
in Proposition
4.1
and the bounds on
q^{R,\epsilon}
found in (
82
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|q^{R,\epsilon}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{2}\left(\frac{q^{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{R,\epsilon}}\right)\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}\right|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\left(T+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{2}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}dxdt\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt.

Using again the bounds in Proposition
4.1
we estimate that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma-1}|\partial_{x}^{2}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|\cdot|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+C_{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt+C_{R,\epsilon}T.

Next, we
observe, due to the definition of
\chi_{R}
and (
83
), that for any
\gamma\geq 1
, we can write:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{\gamma-2}(\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2}

\displaystyle|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|dxdt\leq C_{R}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon}|^{4}+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\|\partial_{x}^{2}\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2},

Finally, we can estimate the quadratic noise term using the noise assumption (
36
), the uniform bounds in Proposition
4.1
, the interpolation inequality (
83
), the properties of the truncation (
18
), and the computation

\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}G_{k}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})=\rho^{R,\epsilon}\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}g_{k}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})+2(1,0)\otimes\nabla_{\rho,q}g_{k}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}),

as follows,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}

\displaystyle|\langle\nabla^{2}_{\rho,q}G^{R,\epsilon}_{k}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\partial_{x}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon}),\partial_{x}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\rangle|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq CA_{0}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})^{2}(1+(\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{2})\Big((\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{4}+(\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon})^{4}\Big)dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{R,\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})\Big((\partial_{x}\rho^{R,\epsilon})^{4}+(\partial_{x}q^{R,\epsilon})^{4}\Big)dxdt\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T,

by the uniform bound on
\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{C(0,T;C(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{R,\epsilon}
and
\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}\|_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}^{2}\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T
in Proposition
4.1
, for a constant
C_{R,\epsilon}
that is independent of
T
. Thus, we conclude that

\frac{\epsilon}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{3}q^{R,\epsilon}|^{2}dxdt\leq C_{R,\epsilon}T.

∎

5.
Invariant measure for the approximate system

In this section, we use the uniform-in-time estimates of Section
4
to show that the approximate system (
20
) has an invariant measure, associated to the Feller semigroup
\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, defined in (
61
) and Proposition
2.4
. We use a standard time-averaging argument to establish existence of an invariant measure to (
20
) by averaging the laws of the stochastic solution
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
to (
20
) with initial condition
(1,0)
over larger and larger time intervals
[0,T]
. This invariant measure corresponds to a stationary solution to the approximate system (
20
), which we can denote by
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
, where we leave the
\epsilon_{N}
dependence implicit. We then use the results on uniform invariant regions (independent of
R
) in Section
3
, to deduce uniform
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
bounds on the stationary solutions
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
independently of the approximation parameters
R
. These uniform estimates (independent of
R
) will help us subsequently pass to the limit in the stationary solutions
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
to the approximate system (
20
), as
R\to\infty
.

5.1.
Existence of an invariant measure.

We will show the existence of an invariant measure to the approximate problem (
20
). To do this, we will define an appropriate path space, which we recall from (
37
):

\mathcal{X}:=\left\{(\rho,q)\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\times H^{2}(\mathbb{T}):\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(x)dx=1\text{ and }\rho\geq\frac{1}{R}\right\}.

We will use a standard Krylov-Bogoliubov (time-averaging) procedure to show the existence of an invariant measure to the approximate system (
20
) on
\mathcal{X}
, associated to the Feller semigroup
\mathcal{P}_{t}
in (
61
), generated by the (Hadamard well-posed) dynamics of the approximate system. See Proposition
2.4
.

Hence, we consider the approximate system (
20
) with initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})=(1,0)
, and we note that there is a unique solution
(\rho^{R,\epsilon},q^{R,\epsilon})
that exists globally in time starting from this initial data
(\rho_{0},(\rho u)_{0})=(1,0)
. We then define the
time-averaged measures
:

(84)

\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}(B)=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{P}\Big((\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t),q^{R,\epsilon}(t))\in B\ \Big|\ (\rho_{0},q_{0})=(1,0)\Big)dt.

We will obtain an invariant measure for the approximate system (
20
) as a weak limit of the measures
\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}
as
T\to\infty
, which will correspond to a statistically stationary solution with paths in
\mathcal{X}
to the approximate problem (
20
). To show that such a weak limit exists, we must show that the time-averaged measures
\{\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}\}_{T\in\mathbb{N}}
are tight as measures on the phase space
\mathcal{X}
, which is the content of the following proposition. The tightness will be a direct consequence of the uniform bounds we have established for the fluid density and the fluid velocity, independently of time.

Proposition 5.1
.

The time-averaged measures
\{\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}\}_{T\in\mathbb{N}}
are tight as measures on
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

We recall from Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
4.2
the following uniform bounds on the density and momentum, where the constants
C
are independent of
T
(but may depend on the parameters
R
and
\epsilon
):

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,\cdot)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}dt\leq C\quad\text{ almost surely},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|q^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}dt\leq C.

Therefore, we define the set

K_{M}:=\left\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\|\rho\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M,\|q\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M\right\}.

and we note that
K_{M}
is a compact subset of the phase space
\mathcal{X}
by standard compact embeddings. We can hence show tightness by considering an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
and showing that for a uniform constant
M
(depending potentially on
\epsilon
), we have that
\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}(K_{M})\geq 1-\varepsilon
for all
T\in\mathbb{N}
. We hence calculate that

\displaystyle\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}(K_{M})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{P}\left(\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M,\|q^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}\leq M\right)dt

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\left[\mathbb{P}\Big(\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}>M\Big)+\mathbb{P}\Big(\|q^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}>M\Big)\right]dt

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{1}{TM^{2}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\Big(\|\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\|q^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{3}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)dt\geq 1-\frac{2C}{M^{2}}.

Thus, choosing
M
sufficiently large, since the constant
C
is independent of
T
in the previous estimate, we obtain the desired tightness result that
\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}(K_{M})\geq 1-\varepsilon
for all
T\in\mathbb{N}
.
∎

Then, we can pass to the weak limit, via a standard Prokhorov theorem argument, in which tightness of probability measures implies weak convergence along a subsequence.

Corollary 5.1
.

For fixed
R,\epsilon>0
, there exists an invariant measure
\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
for
\mathcal{P}_{t}
defined in (
61
), describing the dynamics of (
20
).

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the Krylov–Bogoliubov Theorem (see Theorem 7.1 in
[
16
]
) applied to Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
2.4
.
∎

Using the fact that the time-averaged laws
\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}
defined in (
84
) converge weakly to the invariant measure
\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
, we can deduce properties of the law of the invariant measure. The most important properties for the upcoming analysis are in the following proposition: (1) a uniform
L^{\infty}
bound, which follows from the uniform-in-time bounds for the initial value problem in Proposition
3.1
, and (2) the non-negativity of the density.

Proposition 5.2
.

For the constant
C_{\epsilon}
defined in Proposition
3.1
, depending only on
\epsilon
, the invariant measure
\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
satisfies:

(85)

\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}\Big(\left\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\|(\rho,q)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\right\}\Big)=1.

In addition,

(86)

\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}\left(\left\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\left\|\frac{q}{\rho}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\text{ and }\rho(x)\geq{\frac{1}{R}}\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{T}\right\}\right)=1

and

(87)

\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}\left(\left\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\rho(x)\geq{\frac{1}{R}}\text{ and }\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(x)dx=1\right\}\right)=1.

Proof.

Note that the set
\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\|(\rho,q)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\}
is a closed set in
\mathcal{X}
defined in (
37
), since
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})\subset L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
. Hence, we can use weak convergence of the time-averaged measure
\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}
in (
84
) as
T\to\infty
to the invariant measure
\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
, combined with Portmanteau’s theorem, to obtain the desired result. Namely, for the initial value problem started with initial data
(1,0)
, we have by Proposition
3.1
that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\|(\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t),q^{R,\epsilon}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\Big)=1,\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

Hence, for all
T\in\mathbb{N}
:

\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}\Big(\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\|(\rho,q)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\Big)=1.

So by Portmanteau’s theorem for closed sets:

\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}\Big(\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\|(\rho,q)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\Big)\geq\lim_{T\to\infty}\mu^{R,\epsilon}_{T}\Big(\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\|(\rho,q)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\Big)=1,

which establishes the desired result in (
85
).

To prove the second result in (
86
), note that the set

\left\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:1/R\leq\rho(x)\leq C_{\epsilon}\text{ and }\left\|\frac{q}{\rho}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\right\}

is a closed set in
\mathcal{X}
. So by Proposition
2.2
, Proposition
3.1
, and a similar Portmanteau theorem argument:

\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}\left(\left\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:1/R\leq\rho(x)\leq C_{\epsilon}\text{ and }\left\|\frac{q}{\rho}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}\right\}\right)=1.

This establishes the result in (
86
). Finally, for (
87
), note that

\left\{(\rho,q)\in\mathcal{X}:\rho(x)\geq 1/R\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{T}\text{ and }\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho(x)dx=1\right\}

is a closed set in
\mathcal{X}
, so a similar Portmanteau theorem argument works, once we note that
\rho^{R,\epsilon}(x)\geq 1/R
for all
x\in\mathbb{T}
almost surely by Proposition
2.2
, and furthermore,
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho^{R,\epsilon}(t,x)dx=1
for all
t\geq 0
almost surely.
∎

We can directly translate the existence of an invariant measure
\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
for the approximate system (
20
) into the existence of a statistically stationary solution to the approximate system (
20
).

Corollary 5.2
.

There exists a statistically stationary solution
(\rho^{S}_{R,\epsilon},q^{S}_{R,\epsilon})
for the approximate system (
20
), which furthermore satisfies the uniform bounds almost surely:

(88)

\|(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S},q_{R,\epsilon}^{S})\|_{C({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon},\qquad\left\|\frac{q^{S}_{R,\epsilon}}{\rho^{S}_{R,\epsilon}}\right\|_{C({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon},

for a constant
C_{\epsilon}
, depending only on
\epsilon
, with
\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t,x)\geq 1/R
for all
x\in\mathbb{T}
and
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t,x)dx=1
for all
t\geq 0
, almost surely.

Proof.

To construct the approximate solution, consider a stochastic basis
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0},W)
and (random) initial data
(\rho_{0},q_{0})
with law given by the invariant measure
\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
. Denote the resulting (unique) strong pathwise solution to (
20
) taking continuous paths in
\mathcal{X}
by
(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t),q_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t))
.

By the definition of invariant measure for
\mathcal{P}_{t}
given in (
61
) and the fact that
(\rho_{0},q_{0})\sim\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
, we know that for any
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathcal{X})
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}\varphi(x)\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}(dx)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\mathcal{P}_{t}\varphi(x)\,\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}(dx)=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{P}_{t}\varphi(\rho_{0},q_{0})]=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\rho^{S}_{R,\epsilon}(t),q^{S}_{R,\epsilon}(t))],

which means that
(\rho^{S}_{R,\epsilon}(t),q^{S}_{R,\epsilon}(t))\sim\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
for all
t\geq 0
.

Hence, we have that the resulting solution
(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t),q_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t))
is a statistically stationary solution with continuous paths in
\mathcal{X}
. The bound in
C({\mathbb{R}}_{+};L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}))
follows directly from Proposition
5.2
. We remark that we can establish the uniform lower bound
\rho^{S}_{R,\epsilon}(t,x)\geq 1/R
for
x\in\mathbb{T}
almost surely, where on this almost sure set, this lower bound holds for all
t\geq 0
simultaneously. This is because of the continuity in time
\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}\in C(0,T;\mathcal{X})\subset C(0,T;C(\mathbb{T}))
by Proposition
2.2
.
∎

Finally, we observe the following uniform bound (independent of
R
), which is a consequence of the uniform
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
bound in (
88
). This will be important for obtaining control of the vacuum set where the density is zero, in the subsequent limit passage as
R\to\infty
(see Proposition
6.4
), since the uniform constants in this proposition are independent of
R
.

Proposition 5.3
.

The stationary solution
(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S},q_{R,\epsilon}^{S})
satisfies the following uniform bounds for all
t\geq 0
:

\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{x}(\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t,x)))\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon},\qquad\mathbb{E}\|\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t))\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon},

for a constant
C_{\epsilon}
depending only on
\epsilon
(and not on
R
or
t\geq 0
).

Proof.

By Corollary
5.2
, we can test the continuity equation by
1/\rho_{\epsilon}
since
\rho_{\epsilon}(t,x)\geq 1/R>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{T}
and
t\geq 0
almost surely. We thus obtain for arbitrary
T>0
that for all
t\in[0,T]
:

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{t}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{x}([q_{R,\epsilon}^{S}]_{R})}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}=\epsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{x}^{2}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}.

By integrating by parts:

\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})(T)-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})(0)+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}\cdot\frac{[q_{R,\epsilon}^{S}]_{R}}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}=\epsilon\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\partial_{x}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})^{2}}{(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})^{2}}.

After taking expectation on both sides, we can use stationarity of
(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S},q_{R,\epsilon}^{S})
to obtain for all
t\geq 0
:

\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\partial_{x}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})^{2}}{(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})^{2}}=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}\cdot\frac{[q_{R,\epsilon}^{S}]_{R}}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}.

So by Cauchy’s inequality and the uniform
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
bounds in Proposition
5.2
:

(89)

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\partial_{x}\Big[\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t))\Big]\Big)^{2}=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\partial_{x}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})^{2}}{(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S})^{2}}\leq C_{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left(\frac{[q_{R,\epsilon}^{S}]_{R}}{\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}}\right)^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon}.

This establishes the first estimate. For the second estimate, note that by Corollary
5.2
,
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t,x)dx=1
, so there must exist some point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{T}
such that
\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(x_{0})=1
. Then,

|\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t,x))|^{2}\leq\left|\int_{x_{0}}^{x}\partial_{x}\Big(\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t,x))\Big)dx\right|^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\partial_{x}\Big[\log(\rho_{R,\epsilon}^{S}(t,\cdot))\Big]\Big)^{2},

so the result follows by taking expectations and using the bound (
89
).
∎

6.
Limit passage
R\to\infty
.

In the previous section, we obtained an invariant measure for the approximate system (
20
). This invariant measure corresponds to a (stochastic) stationary solution, see Proposition
5.2
. While these solutions depend also on the parameter
\epsilon
, we omit the explicit dependence on
\epsilon
in this section for convenience of notation, as we focus on passing
R\to\infty
for fixed but arbitrary
\epsilon>0
. That is,

Notation 6.1
.

We denote by
\boldsymbol{U}_{R}=(\rho_{R},q_{R})
the stationary solution constructed in Corollary
5.2
(denoted earlier by
(\rho^{S}_{R,\epsilon},q^{S}_{R,\epsilon})
) which is the strong pathwise solution to (
20
) whose law at every time
t\geq 0
is given by
the invariant measure
\mathcal{L}^{R,\epsilon}_{\rho,q}
constructed in Corollary
5.1
.

Most of the estimates obtained in the previous section depended on the parameter
R
. To prepare for the limit passage as
R\to\infty
, we thus need to obtain estimates on the approximate solutions
({\rho}_{R},{q}_{R})
independently of
R
. Note that upon obtaining the statistically stationary solution, the information about initial conditions is lost, as it no longer makes sense to talk about an initial value problem (since these statistically stationary solutions are obtained by time averaging). However, we will often use the fact that the law of the solution is equal for all times to recover uniform bounds on the approximate statistically stationary solutions.

In addition, we note that the approximation scheme (
20
) at the level of
R
is still in conservation form. Namely, we note that an entropy flux pair
(\eta,H)
for the original system (
6
) corresponds to an entropy flux pair
\Big(\eta,\chi_{R}({\rho,q})H\Big)
for the approximate system (
20
). Hence, the approximate problem has the following entropy equality, which is satisfied for all entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
for the original problem, and for all test functions
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and nonnegative
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(0,\infty)
:

(90)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{R}(t))\varphi(x)dx\right)\partial_{t}\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}H(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})\partial_{x}\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\\
-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha q_{R}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dW(t)\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})G^{2}_{R,\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)=\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{R},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{R}\rangle\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\\
-\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{R})\partial^{2}_{x}\varphi dx\right)\psi(t)dt.

Our goal is to use uniform bounds on the solutions (independent of the parameter
R
) in order to pass to the limit as
R\to\infty
in the approximate solutions
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
and also in the approximate entropy equality (
90
).

We aim to obtain almost sure strong convergence as
R\to\infty
for our approximate stationary solutions. For that purpose, we will apply Jakubowski’s version of the classical Skorohod representation theorem
[
30
]
.

Consider the phase space:

(91)

\begin{split}\mathcal{S}:=[C_{\text{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap L^{2}_{\text{loc}}([0,\infty);H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))&\cap(L^{2}_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{2}(\mathbb{T})),w)]^{2}\\
&\times(L^{2}_{loc}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T})),w)\times C(0,\infty;\mathcal{{U}}),\end{split}

and let
\mu_{R}
denote the law of the random variable

\mu_{R}=\text{Law}_{\mathcal{S}}\left(\rho_{R},q_{R},\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{R}}{\rho_{R}},W\right),

in
\mathcal{S}
, where
\boldsymbol{U}_{R}:=(\rho_{R},q_{R})
. Here,
(X,w)
denotes the space
X
equipped with the weak topology. The goal will be to show that the laws
\mu_{R}
of the approximate statistically stationary solutions are tight as probability measures on the phase space
\mathcal{S}
.

6.1.
Uniform bounds on
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
in
R
.

To show tightness of the laws
\mu_{R}
, we will establish uniform bounds on the approximate statistically stationary solutions
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
, independently of
R
. We first show that the following energy bounds are satisfied by the approximate statistically stationary solutions.

Proposition 6.1
.

For any
T>0
the stationary solution
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
to (
20
) satisfies the following bounds, where
C_{\epsilon,T}
depends only on
\epsilon>0
and
T>0
, and is independent of
R
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|q_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon,T}\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{R}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}{\text{ for every $t\geq 0$}.}

Proof.

The proof for
\mathbb{E}\|q_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon,T}
follows identically the proof of the bounds found in (
80
) which are independent of
R
.

We test (
20
)
2
by
\rho_{R}
and apply Corollary
5.2
:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\rho_{R}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}+\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho,q)q_{R}(t)\partial_{x}\rho_{R}(t)dx

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{T}}q_{R}^{2}(t)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}(t)|^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}(t)|^{2}.

We apply expectation on both sides and use stationarity of
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
to obtain for any
t\geq 0
that

\displaystyle\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}(t)|^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon}.

∎

We will next upgrade these bounds and obtain the following tightness result for the approximate laws
\mu_{R}
in
\mathcal{S}
, defined in (
91
).

Proposition 6.2
.

For any fixed
\epsilon>0
, the laws
\mu_{R}
are tight in the path space
\mathcal{S}
, and hence,
\mu_{R}
converges weakly along a subsequence as
R\to\infty
to some limiting probability measure
\mu_{\epsilon}
on
\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Part 1: Tightness of the laws of
\rho_{R}
:
We will begin by proving that for any
T>0
:

(92)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon,T},

(93)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{C^{0,\frac{1}{4}}(0,T;H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon,T},

for some constant
C_{\epsilon,T}>0
independent of
R
.

Observe as before (see the proof of Proposition
4.1
), that using maximal regularity for
\rho_{R}
satisfying (
20
)
1
and applying the bounds found in Proposition
6.1
, we have that for some
C_{\epsilon,T}>0
depending only on
\epsilon
and
T
:

(94)

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{t}\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}+\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{xx}\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}&\leq C\Big(\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{R}(0,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}+\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{x}q_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\Big)\leq C_{\epsilon,T},\end{split}

where we additionally used Proposition
6.1
. This finishes the proof of (
92
).
Hence, thanks to Sobolev interpolating inequalities, we have,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{C^{0,\frac{1}{4}}(0,T;H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{H^{\frac{3}{4}}(0,T;H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\|\rho_{R}\|^{\frac{3}{2}}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\|\rho_{R}\|^{\frac{1}{2}}_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\|\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\right)^{\frac{3}{4}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}\leq C_{\epsilon,T}.

This finishes the proof of (
93
) which is key in proving the tightness result for the laws of
\rho_{R}
because of the following compact embedding, which follows from the Arzela-Ascoli theorem:

C^{0,\frac{1}{4}}(0,T;H^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{T}))\subset\subset C(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T})).

Recall that a set
K
is compact in
C_{\text{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
if
K_{T}=\{f|_{[0,T]};f\in K\}
is compact in
C(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
for every
T\in\mathbb{N}
. This fact follows from a diagonalization argument i.e. by obtaining a subsequence
\{f_{j_{k}}^{1}\}
converging in
K|_{[0,1]}
of a sequence
\{f_{j}\}
bounded in
K
and then thinning it for every
T\in\mathbb{N}
to extract a subsequence of
\{f_{j}\}
converging in
C_{\text{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
.
Hence, we obtain tightness of the laws of
\rho_{R}
in
C_{\text{loc}}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
by an application of the Chebyshev inequality. The same is true for tightness of the laws of
\rho_{R}
in
L^{2}_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))
thanks to the Aubin-Lions theorem that states that

L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\subset\subset L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T})).

Finally, tightness of laws in
(L^{2}_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{2}(\mathbb{T})),w)
is immediate from the uniform bounds in (
92
).

Part 2: Tightness of the laws of
q_{R}
:

To prove the second half of the tightness result, we will prove that for any
T>0
, we have

(95)
