\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|q_{R}(t)\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}+\mathbb{E}\|q_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon,T},

where the constant
C_{\epsilon,T}
is independent of
R
.

We recall (
81
) and take
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}
and then expectation on both sides. We will next estimate each term on the right hand side, independently of
R
, of the resulting equation.
For that purpose, we will appeal to the uniform bounds obtained in Corollary
5.2
and the energy bounds Proposition
6.1
. For the first term we thus obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\chi_{R}({\rho_{R},q_{R}})\partial_{x}q_{R}\left(\frac{q_{R}}{\rho_{R}}\right)\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}\right|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}dxdt+C_{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}q_{R}|^{2}\left(\frac{q_{R}}{\rho_{R}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,T}+\frac{\epsilon}{4}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}dx.

Similarly, by Corollary
5.2
and Proposition
6.1
, for the second term we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|q_{R}\chi_{R}({\rho_{R},q_{R}})\partial_{x}\left(\frac{q_{R}}{\rho_{R}}\right)\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}\right|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\frac{q_{R}\partial_{x}q_{R}}{\rho_{R}}-\frac{q_{R}^{2}\partial_{x}\rho}{\rho^{2}_{R}}\right|^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}q_{R}|^{2}+|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}|^{2}dx+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,T}+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}dx.

Next, we obtain the following estimate for any
\gamma\geq 1
using Corollary
5.2
and Proposition
6.1
:

\displaystyle\kappa\gamma\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\left|\rho_{R}^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\rho_{R}\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}\right|dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}dxdt+C_{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{R}^{2(\gamma-1)}|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}dxdt+C_{\epsilon,T}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon,T}+\frac{\epsilon}{6}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\partial_{x}^{2}q_{R}|^{2}dxdt.

Finally, we recall that
\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}=\rho\nabla_{\rho,q}g_{k}^{R,\epsilon}+(1,0)g_{k}^{R,\epsilon}
and we use (
36
), in addition to Corollary
5.2
and Proposition
6.1
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho_{R},q_{R})\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho_{R},q_{R})|^{2}dxdt

\displaystyle\leq 2A_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(1+\rho_{R}^{2})\Big(|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}|^{2}+|\partial_{x}q_{R}|^{2}\Big)dxdt

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}T.

The final martingale term is treated by using the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho,q)q_{x}dxdW_{k}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\chi_{R}(\rho,q)\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho,q)q_{x}\right)^{2}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\Big\|\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho,q)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\cdot\|q_{x}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\right)^{2}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|q_{x}(t)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Big\|\nabla_{\rho,q}G_{k}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho,q)\cdot\partial_{x}(\rho,q)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}dt\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|q_{x}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\right)+8A_{0}^{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}(1+\rho_{R}^{2})\Big(|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}|^{2}+|\partial_{x}q_{R}|^{2}\Big)dxdt

(96)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|q_{x}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\right)+C_{\epsilon,T}.

Thus, we conclude the proof of (
95
) by using the equation (
81
) with supremum in time
t\in[0,T]
and expectation applied to both sides, where we move the term
\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{E}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|q_{x}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\right)
to the other side. Now we consider the approximate momentum equation in its integral form,

\displaystyle q_{R}(t)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{R}(\rho_{R},q_{R})\left(2\partial_{x}q_{R}\left(\frac{q_{R}}{\rho_{R}}\right)-\partial_{x}\rho_{R}\left(\frac{q_{R}^{2}}{\rho^{2}_{R}}\right)\right)dt+\kappa\gamma\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{R}(\rho_{R},q_{R})\rho_{R}^{\gamma-1}\partial_{x}\rho_{R}dt

\displaystyle-\epsilon\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{xx}q_{R}dt+\int_{0}^{t}\chi_{R}\left(\rho_{R},q_{R}\right)\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{R,\epsilon}(\rho_{R},q_{R})dW=:I_{1}(t)+I_{2}(t)+I_{3}(t)+I_{4}(t).

We will first show that the integrals
I_{i};i=1,2,3
are bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega,H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T})))
.
Again, we apply Corollary
5.2
and the energy bounds
\mathbb{E}\|q_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}+\mathbb{E}\|\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon}
from Proposition
6.1
to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{t}I_{1}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{x}q_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\left\|\frac{q_{R}}{\rho_{R}}\right\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}))}

\displaystyle\qquad+C\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\left\|\frac{q_{R}}{\rho_{R}}\right\|^{4}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon,T}.

Similarly for any
\gamma\geq 1
, we observe

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{t}I_{2}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{x}\rho_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\|\rho_{R}^{\gamma-1}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{T}.

Now we estimate
I_{3}
by using (
95
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{t}I_{3}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq\epsilon\mathbb{E}\|\partial_{xx}q_{R}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\epsilon,T}.

For the stochastic integral
I_{4}
, we use the fact that for any
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
,
q\geq 2
and Hilbert space
H
(see Lemma 2.1 in
[
24
]
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\|\int_{0}^{\cdot}\Phi dW\right\|^{q}_{W^{\beta,q}(0,T;H)}\leq C\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\Phi\|^{q}_{L_{2}(\mathcal{U},H)}dt.

Then, Corollary
5.2
again and (
36
) give us that for any
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
and
q\geq 2
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|I_{4}\|^{q}_{W^{\beta,q}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\boldsymbol{G}^{R,\epsilon}\|^{q}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq CA_{0}^{q}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\|\rho_{R,\epsilon}\|^{q}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon,T}.

Hence, we define the following set for the stochastic integral:

\mathcal{B}^{M}_{S}:=\left\{\,Y\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\;:\;\|Y\|_{W^{\beta,4}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq M\right\},

for any
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
. We also define the following set for the deterministic terms:

\mathcal{B}^{M}_{D}:=\left\{\,X\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\;:\;\|X\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq M\right\}.

For
q_{R}
, we define the set
\mathcal{B}^{M}:=\mathcal{B}^{M}_{S}+\mathcal{B}^{M}_{D}
.
That is,

\displaystyle\{q_{R}\in\mathcal{B}^{M}\}\supset\{I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}\in\mathcal{B}^{M}_{D}\}\cap\{I_{4}\in\mathcal{B}^{M}_{S}\}.

Hence, for
\frac{1}{4}<\beta<\frac{1}{2}
, so that
4\beta>1
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}(\{q_{R}\notin\mathcal{B}^{M}\})

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}(\{\|I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}>M\})+{\mathbb{P}}(\{\|I_{4}\|_{W^{\beta,4}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}>M\})

(97)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{E}\|I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}\|_{H^{1}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}+\frac{1}{M}\mathbb{E}\|I_{4}\|_{W^{\beta,4}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq\frac{C_{\epsilon,T}}{M}.

Thus, the tightness of the laws of
q_{R}
in the function space defined in
\mathcal{S}
follows from the preceding calculations in (
6.1
) and (
95
), since
\mathcal{B}^{M}
is compact in
C_{loc}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
due to (see e.g.
[
12
,
43
]
):

C(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))\cap W^{\beta,q}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\subset\subset C(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T})),\qquad\text{for $0<\beta<1,q>1$ such that $\beta q>1$}.

Similarly, tightness of the laws of
q_{R}
in
L^{2}_{loc}(0,\infty;H^{1}(\mathbb{T}))
, is due to the Aubin-Lions-Simon theorem which, for any
0<\beta<1
, states that

L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap H^{\beta}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))\subset\subset L^{2}(0,T;H^{1}(\mathbb{T})).

The uniform bounds in (
92
) and (
95
) also give tightness of
(\rho_{R},q_{R})
in
((L^{2}(0,T;H^{2}(\mathbb{T}))^{2},w)
immediately, see (
91
). Finally, to observe tightness of the laws of
\rho_{R}^{-1}\partial_{x}\rho_{R}
in
(L^{2}_{loc}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T})),w)
, we note that from Proposition
5.3
, we have that for each
T>0
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\partial_{x}\rho_{R})^{2}}{\rho_{R}^{2}}dxdt\leq C_{\epsilon}T,

for a constant
C_{\epsilon}
independent of
R
.

∎

6.2.
Skorohod representation theorem and limit passage as
R\to\infty
.

Given the tightness result in Proposition
6.2
, we can obtain almost sure convergence of our approximate solutions, with the trade off of transferring to a potentially different probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
but with equivalence of laws. In particular, by the classical Skorohod representation theorem, we can construct new random variables
(\tilde{\rho}_{R},\tilde{q}_{R},\tilde{\ell}_{R},\tilde{W}_{R})
on a new probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})=([0,1),\text{Borel}([0,1)),\text{Leb}([0,1))
with the same laws as
(\rho_{R},q_{R},\rho_{R}^{-1}\partial_{x}\rho_{R},W)
in
\mathcal{S}
as defined in (
91
), such that for any fixed
\epsilon>0

(98)

(\tilde{\rho}_{R},\tilde{q}_{R},\tilde{\ell}_{R},\tilde{W}_{R})\to({\rho}_{\epsilon},{q}_{\epsilon},\ell_{\epsilon},{W}_{\epsilon}),\qquad\text{ in }\mathcal{S},\text{ $\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely,}

where the limiting random variable
({\rho}_{\epsilon},{u}_{\epsilon},\ell_{\epsilon},{W}_{\epsilon})
on the new probability space has law given by
\mu_{\epsilon}
, which is identified as the weak limit of the laws
\mu_{R}
(along a subsequence), as in Proposition
6.2
. See (
91
) for the definition of the phase space
\mathcal{S}
.

We will now deduce several consequences of the convergence (
98
) that will help us pass to the limit as
R\to\infty
in the approximate entropy balance equation (
90
). Let,

\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}:=(\tilde{\rho}_{R},\tilde{q}_{R})\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}:=(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon}).

Proposition 6.3
.

For almost every
(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
, we have
that

\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\to\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\to\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\quad\text{ a.e. in }\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}.

Proof.

We first show that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}\to\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}
, up to a subsequence, for almost every
(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
. Since
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}\to\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}
in
C_{loc}([0,\infty);L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))
,
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely, we have that
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}\to\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}
,
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely in
L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
for all
T>0
. Furthermore, by Proposition
6.1
and equivalence of laws (by the Skorohod representation theorem), we have that for a uniform constant
C_{T}
depending only on
T>0
and
\epsilon>0
(but independent of
R>0
):

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|(\tilde{\rho}_{R},\tilde{q}_{R})\|_{L^{1}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\|(\tilde{\rho}_{R},\tilde{q}_{R})\|_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq C_{T,\epsilon}.

So by Vitali’s convergence theorem,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}\to\boldsymbol{U}_{R}
in
L^{1}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
for all
T>0
, which establishes the desired result.

To show the result for the first spatial derivatives, namely that
\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\to\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)
for almost every
(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}
, we can use the same Vitali convergence argument to show that
\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}\to\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}
in
L^{1}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
for all
T>0
. ∎

As a consequence of this almost everywhere convergence result in Proposition
6.3
, we prove that there is no vacuum in the limiting system.

Proposition 6.4
.

For every fixed but arbitrary
\epsilon>0
, the stationary solution
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
satisfies
\rho_{\epsilon}>0
almost everywhere on
\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
. Furthermore,

(99)

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\log(\rho_{\epsilon})|^{2}dxdt\leq C_{\epsilon}T,

Proof.

Note that by Proposition
5.3
, we have that for all
R>0
, almost surely:

{\mathbb{E}}\|\log(\rho_{R}(t))\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}\leq C_{\epsilon},\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

So taking a time integral and transferring to the new probability space by equivalence of laws:

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\log(\tilde{\rho}_{R})\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}dxdt\leq C_{\epsilon}T\qquad\text{ and hence}\qquad\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\log(\tilde{\rho}_{R})|^{2}dxdt\leq C_{\epsilon}T,

for all
T>0
. So (
99
) follows from Proposition
6.3
and Fatou’s lemma. ∎

Remark 6.1
.

The observation that
\rho_{\epsilon}>0
almost everywhere on
\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
, which follows from (
99
), is also essential for ensuring that the terms in the entropy inequality, such as the expectation of the dissipation integral involving
D^{2}\eta
which is continuous away from the vacuum set
\rho=0
, can be properly defined.

Thanks to the previous two propositions, we can now identify the new random variables
\tilde{\ell}_{R}
and
\ell_{\epsilon}
.

Corollary 6.1
.

For the new random variable we have

\tilde{\ell}_{R}=\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}},\qquad\ell_{\epsilon}=\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}.

Proof.

From equivalence of laws
\displaystyle\left(\rho_{R},\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{R}}{\rho_{R}}\right)=_{d}(\tilde{\rho}_{R},\tilde{\ell}_{R})
, it is immediate that
\displaystyle\tilde{\ell}_{R}=\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}
. Furthermore, we know from (
98
) that

\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}\rightharpoonup\ell_{\epsilon},\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely and weakly in $L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\mathbb{T}))$}.

Then Propositions
6.3
and
6.4
allow us to uniquely identify the weak
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost sure limit as
\displaystyle\ell_{\epsilon}=\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}
.
∎

As another consequence that will be important in the limit passage as
R\to\infty
(in particular, for passing to the limit in the entropy dissipation terms), we observe the following convergence.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{R}=\frac{\tilde{q}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}
and
\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}=\frac{q_{\epsilon}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}
. We have the following
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost sure strong convergence:

(100)

\tilde{u}_{R}\to u_{\epsilon},\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely and strongly in $L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})$}.

Consequently, we have that for arbitrary
T>0
:

(101)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\partial_{x}\tilde{q}_{R})^{2}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}dxdt\to\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\partial_{x}q_{\epsilon})^{2}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}dxdt,\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely.}

Proof.

By the vacuum estimates in Propositions
5.3
and
6.4
, and the almost everywhere convergence in Proposition
6.3
, we have that

\tilde{u}_{R}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\to u_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\omega},t,x),\qquad\text{ for almost every $(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}$}

Furthermore, by Corollary
5.2
and equivalence of laws:

\|\tilde{u}_{R}\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon},\qquad\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{\epsilon}.

So by dominated convergence theorem,

\tilde{u}_{R}\to u_{\epsilon},\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely and strongly in $L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})$},

Hence, we immediately conclude from the weak convergence (
98
) and Corollary
6.1
:

(102)

\tilde{u}_{R}\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}\rightharpoonup u_{\epsilon}\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}}{\rho_{\epsilon}},\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely and weakly in $L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})$.}

Finally, we establish the convergence in (
101
). By integration by parts, we compute that

(103)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{(\partial_{x}\tilde{q}_{R})^{2}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}dxdt=-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tilde{u}_{R}\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{q}_{R}dxdt+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tilde{u}_{R}\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}\partial_{x}\tilde{q}_{R}dxdt.

We can then pass to the limit in each term. Using the strong convergence from (
100
) and the weak convergence from (
98
) of
\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{q}_{R}\to\partial_{x}^{2}q_{\epsilon}
in
L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
,
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely, we obtain:

(104)

-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tilde{u}_{R}\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{q}_{R}dxdt\to-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}q_{\epsilon}dxdt,\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely.}

The convergence (
102
) implies that,

(105)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\tilde{u}_{R}\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}dxdt\to\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}u_{\epsilon}\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}dxdt,\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely.}

Using the convergences (
104
) and (
105
) in (
103
), we obtain the desired convergence in (
101
).
∎

We now have all of the components needed to pass to the limit as
R\to\infty
in the entropy equality (
90
) to obtain a limiting entropy equality for
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
.

Proposition 6.6
.

For every
\epsilon>0
, the limiting random variable
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
with continuous paths in
\mathcal{X}
almost surely, is a stationary martingale solution to

(106)

\begin{cases}\partial_{t}\rho_{\epsilon}+\partial_{x}q_{\epsilon}=\epsilon\Delta\rho_{\epsilon},\\
dq_{\epsilon}+\partial_{x}\left(\frac{q_{\epsilon}^{2}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}\right)+\partial_{x}(\kappa\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma})=\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon}(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})dW_{\epsilon}+\epsilon\Delta q_{\epsilon}.\end{cases}

Moreover, the solution
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
satisfies the entropy equality for all entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
generated by subpolynomial
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
, and for all deterministic nonnegative test functions
\varphi(x)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and
\psi(t)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,\infty)
with
\psi\geq 0
:

(107)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}(t))\varphi(x)dx\right)\partial_{t}\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}H(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\\
-\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha q_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\Phi(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dW_{\epsilon}(t)\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt=\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\\
-\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi dx\right)\psi(t)dt.

Proof.

This follows from passing to the limit
R\to\infty
in (
20
) and using the fact that the notion of stationarity
is stable under strong (and weak) convergence as proven in Lemmas A.5 (and A.4) in
[
9
]
. We only make comments about passing to the limit in the dissipation term, as
R\to\infty
, namely:

(108)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R})\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R},\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}\rangle\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\to\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt,

almost surely as
R\to\infty
. For all other terms in the entropy equality, we can pass to the limit as
R\to\infty
using the uniform
L^{\infty}
bounds in Corollary
5.2
, the almost everywhere convergence in Proposition
6.3
, the entropy bounds in Proposition
1.3
(combined with the
L^{\infty}
bounds in Corollary
5.2
and the fact that the vacuum is of measure zero as proved in in Propositions
5.3
and
6.4
), and the dominated convergence theorem.

Hence, it suffices to show the convergence (
108
),
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely. We can verify this convergence by using the generalized dominated convergence theorem (see Theorem 11 in Section 4.4 of
[
42
]
). Fix some deterministic nonnegative functions
\varphi(x)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
and
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,\infty))
, and fix some
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
that defines some entropy
\eta
. Recall from Corollary
5.2
and by equivalence of laws and Proposition
6.4
, that for some deterministic constant
C_{\epsilon}>0
,

\frac{1}{R}\leq\tilde{\rho}_{R}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\leq C_{\epsilon},\qquad 0<\rho_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\leq C_{\epsilon},\qquad\text{ for almost every }(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}.

Hence, from Proposition
1.3
,

\|D^{2}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R})\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{g,\epsilon}\tilde{\rho}_{R}^{-1},\qquad\|D^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}\leq C_{g,\epsilon}\rho_{\epsilon}^{-1},\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely.}

Hence, choosing
T
so that
\text{supp}(\psi(t))\subset[0,T]
, we note that for almost every
(\tilde{\omega},t,x)\in\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}
:

(109)

|\langle D^{2}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R})\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R},\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}\rangle\varphi(x)\psi(t)dt|\leq 2C_{\epsilon,g}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}}\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}\cdot\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R},

and similarly for the limiting
\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}
. By the weak convergence

\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}\rightharpoonup\frac{\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}}{\rho_{\epsilon}},\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely in $L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})$}

and the strong convergence

\partial_{x}\tilde{\rho}_{R}\to\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon},\qquad\text{$\tilde{\mathbb{P}}$-almost surely in $L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T}$)}

from (
98
), and also by (
101
) from Proposition
6.5
, we conclude that

(110)

2C_{\epsilon,g}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}}{\tilde{\rho}_{R}}\cdot\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{R}dxdt\to 2C_{\epsilon,g}\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}\cdot\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}dxdt,

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely. So by applying the generalized dominated convergence theorem (see Theorem 11 in Section 4.4 of
[
42
]
) in the
(t,x)
variables (pathwise in outcome
\tilde{\omega}
) using (
109
) and (
110
), we obtain the desired
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost sure convergence in (
108
).

∎

7.
Uniform bounds:
\epsilon_{N}
level

Our goal in this section will be to pass to the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
in the statistically stationary solutions to the approximate problem (
106
) with artificial viscosity. At this stage, we have a statistically stationary solution
\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}:=(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
defined on
[0,\infty)
that satisfies the
approximate entropy equality
(
107
).
Using stationarity (the equivalence of laws of the process
\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}(t)
at all times
t\geq 0
), we can deduce uniform bounds, where usual dissipative terms in the energy inequality become
L^{\infty}
in time bounds. One particular challenge here is that the
\rho\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})
term for entropies of the form
\eta_{m}
, does not immediately give any bounds on higher powers of
\rho_{\epsilon}
. We will hence have to use a Bogovskii operator technique to obtain higher integrability, along with carefully managing numerology of powers of
\rho_{\epsilon}
in order to close the resulting estimate. This is done in Proposition
7.1
. From there, we will deduce further uniform bounds independent of
\epsilon
in Section
7.2
, that will be important for the
\epsilon_{N}
limit passage in the next section. Note that as discussed in Section
2.2
, it is easiest to define
\epsilon
-level approximations of the noise using a specific sequence
\{\epsilon_{N}\}_{N=1}^{\infty}
with
\epsilon_{N}\searrow 0
, but for simplicity of notation, we will omit the subscript of
N
on the
\epsilon
parameters in this section.

7.1.
Entropy bounds uniform in
\epsilon
.

In this subsection, we deduce uniform entropy bounds independently of
\epsilon
. This is the crucial component of the proof, as uniform bounds that we derived previously, such as the uniform
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})
bounds on
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
, are
\epsilon
dependent. This is done as follows. From stationarity, we have a bound on the dissipation rate uniformly in
\epsilon
. We can then use these uniform bounds to recover a moment estimate on the powers of the density. Note that the dissipative term in the approximate entropy inequality involves
q\partial_{q}\eta
, and since this term does not include any terms with just powers of
\rho
by themselves (see Proposition
1.1
), we do not immediately have these bounds for all powers of the density. However, we can use a Bogovskii-type approach to recover these higher moment bounds. This is similar in spirit to what is done for compressible Navier-Stokes equations, except for this case, without the additional in
\epsilon
bounds on
\|u_{\epsilon}\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{T})}
.

Proposition 7.1
.

The approximate solutions
\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}
satisfy the following uniform bounds for all positive integers
m
:

(111)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dx\leq C_{m},

for a constant
C_{m}
that is independent of
\epsilon
and
t\geq 0
, which depends only on
m
and the damping parameter
\alpha>0
.
Consequently, the bounds above imply that,

(112)

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}\Big(u_{\epsilon}^{2m}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\Big)\leq C_{m},\qquad{\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+m(\gamma-1)}\leq C_{m},

(113)

\epsilon{\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(u_{\epsilon}^{2(m-1)}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}\Big)\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma-2}|\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}|^{2}+\epsilon{\mathbb{E}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(u_{\epsilon}^{2(m-1)}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}\Big)\rho_{\epsilon}|\partial_{x}u_{\epsilon}|^{2}\leq C_{m}.

Proof.

We use the approximate entropy equality with the entropy
\eta_{m}
for arbitrary positive integers
m
and test functions
\varphi(x)=1
and
\psi(t)=\mathbbm{1}_{[T,T+1]}(t)
. We obtain after taking expectations:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}(T+1))

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\int_{T}^{T+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha q_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dxdt+\mathbb{E}\int_{T}^{T+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\epsilon\langle D^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle dxdt

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}(T))+\mathbb{E}\int_{T}^{T+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dxdt.

Then, by using stationarity and the fact that
D^{2}\eta_{m}
is positive semidefinite, we obtain using the assumption on the noise (
4
) and Proposition
1.1
that:

\displaystyle\alpha c_{m}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}\Big(u_{\epsilon}^{2m}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\Big)dx

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha q_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dx

(114)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dx\leq C_{m}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}),

and similarly,

(115)

\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle\leq C_{m}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}).

Note, due to (
7.1
) and (
115
) and the explicit expression for
\langle D^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle
, that (
112
) and (
113
) will follow for every integer
m\geq 0
if we prove the bound in (
111
) for every integer
m\geq 0
.
This will be done via induction.
For the inductive step, we assume that for some positive integer
m
, we have

(116)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{j}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\leq C_{m},\qquad\forall 0\leq j\leq m

and then prove that the same holds when
m
is replaced by
m+1
. Note that the base case
m=0
of (
116
) is true since
\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})=\rho
and so by conservation of mass,
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})=M
.

First, thanks to (
7.1
) and (
115
) and the algebraic bound on the entropy dissipation in Proposition
1.2
, the assumption (
116
) also implies that we have the following bounds for all
0\leq j\leq m
:

(117)

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}\Big(u_{\epsilon}^{2(j+1)}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{j(\gamma-1)}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\Big)dx\leq C_{m},\qquad\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+j(\gamma-1)}dx\leq C_{m}\\
\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(u_{\epsilon}^{2j}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{j(\gamma-1)}\Big)\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma-2}|\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}|^{2}+\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(u_{\epsilon}^{2j}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{j(\gamma-1)}\Big)\rho_{\epsilon}|\partial_{x}u_{\epsilon}|^{2}\leq C_{m}.

Before we proceed, we observe that Proposition
1.1
and (
7.1
) applied recursively gives us,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2(m+1)}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+(m+1)(\gamma-1)}\leq C_{m}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})+C_{m}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+(m+1)(\gamma-1)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})+C_{m}\sum_{k=1}^{m+1}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+k(\gamma-1)}\leq C_{m,M}\left(1+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+(m+1)(\gamma-1)}\right).

Hence, our
aim
above boils down to proving that
\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+(m+1)(\gamma-1)}
is bounded under the assumption (
116
) (and thus (
117
)).

The idea behind showing the inductive step is to test the weak formulation with a special test function
\varphi
(see (
119
)) constructed using the Bogovskii operator. We use the entropy-flux pair:
\eta=\rho u
,
H=\rho u^{2}+\kappa\rho^{\gamma}
in (
107
) to obtain the following weak formulation for any deterministic test function
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T})
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}(T+1)\varphi-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}(T)\varphi\\
=\mathbb{E}\int_{T}^{T+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\partial_{x}\varphi dxdt+\mathbb{E}\int_{T}^{T+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}{\kappa}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\partial_{x}\varphi-\mathbb{E}\int_{T}^{T+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\varphi(x)dxdt+\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{T}^{T+1}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\partial^{2}_{x}\varphi.

Then, by stationarity, we have that for all
t\geq 0
:

(118)

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\kappa\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\partial_{x}\varphi=-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\partial_{x}\varphi dxdt+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\varphi(x)dx-\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\partial^{2}_{x}\varphi.

To obtain a higher moment estimate on the density, we will use the Bogovskii operator in 1D and substitute the following test function:

(119)

\varphi=\int_{0}^{x}\left(\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)}dx\right)dx.

Since

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\kappa\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\partial_{x}\left(\int_{0}^{x}\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)}\right)=\kappa\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)},

this gives us, for
s=m(\gamma-1)

(120)

\begin{split}\kappa\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+(m+1)(\gamma-1)}dx&=\kappa\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)}dx\\
&=\kappa\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s}\right)dx-\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+s}u_{\epsilon}^{2}dx\\
&+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s}\right)dx+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\varphi(x)dx-\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}(\rho_{\epsilon}^{s})dx\\
&:=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}+I_{5}.\end{split}

We estimate the terms on the right-hand side of (
120
) under the assumption (
116
) as follows.

Term I1.

For Term I1, we claim that for any
s>0
we have the estimate:

(121)

|I_{1}|\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)}dx+C_{m,\delta}.

If
0<s\leq 1
, we can use the fact that
\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s}dx\leq M^{s}
almost surely, by conservation of mass and Young’s inequality to immediately deduce this inequality.

In the case where
s>1
, we establish (
121
) by considering
r>1
to be chosen later, and estimating
I_{1}
as follows:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s}\right)dx\leq M^{(r-1)/r}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{{sr-(r-1)}}\right)^{1/r}dx=C_{r}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{sr-(r-1)}\right)^{1/r}\right]\\
\leq\frac{\delta}{2}\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\right)^{\frac{p}{p-1}}+C_{\delta}{{\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{sr-{(r-1)}}dx\right)^{p/r}}}.

Let
\displaystyle p=\frac{\gamma+s}{s}
and
\displaystyle\frac{p}{p-1}=\frac{\gamma+s}{\gamma}
and then choose
r
so that
1<\displaystyle r<p=\frac{\gamma+s}{s}
. We thus conclude that

|I_{1}|\leq\frac{\delta}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+s}+C_{\delta}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+s-\frac{p}{r}{(r-1)}}\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+s}+C_{\delta},

by Young’s inequality
\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+s-\frac{p{(r-1)}}{r}}\leq\frac{\delta}{2}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+s}+C_{\delta}
, where we note that this inequality applies since
s,r>1
and hence the exponent is positive:
\displaystyle\gamma+s-\frac{p}{r}{(r-1)}\geq\gamma-\frac{\gamma}{s}+s-1>0
.

Term I2.
Observe that, since
s=m(\gamma-1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+s}u_{\epsilon}^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\frac{1}{m+1}}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s+\frac{m}{m+1}}\leq C_{\delta}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2(m+1)}dx+{\delta}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+\frac{(m+1)s}{m}}dx

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2(m+1)}dx+{\delta}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)+\gamma}dx

\displaystyle\leq C_{m,\delta}+{\delta}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)+\gamma}dx,

thanks to our induction assumption (
117
).

Term I3.
We estimate again thanks to (
117
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho^{s}_{\epsilon}\right)dx

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s}dx\right)\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}dx\right)^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2(m+1)}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{m+1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq M^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2(m+1)}dx+\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s}dx\right)^{\frac{m+1}{m}}\right)

\displaystyle{{\leq C\left(1+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\frac{(m+1)s}{m}}dx\right).}}

Since

(122)

\displaystyle\frac{(m+1)s}{m}=(m+1)(\gamma-1)=m(\gamma-1)+\gamma-1<m(\gamma-1)+\gamma,

we have by Young’s inequality:

|I_{3}|\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)}dx+C_{\delta}.

Term I4.
We compute for
s=m(\gamma-1)
that

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\varphi(x)dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{m+1}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{m+1}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}dx\right)^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\right)

\displaystyle\leq M^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\mathbb{E}\left(\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{m+1}dx\right)^{\frac{1}{m+1}}\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\right)

\displaystyle{{\leq M^{\frac{m}{m+1}}\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{m+1}dx+\mathbb{E}\Big(\|\varphi\|^{\frac{m+1}{m}}_{W^{1,(m+1)/m}(\mathbb{T})}\Big)\right)}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}\left(1+\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\frac{(m+1)s}{m}}dx\right),

where we used the inductive assumption (
117
) and the following

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{W^{1,(m+1)/m}}^{\frac{m+1}{m}}=\left\|\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)}-\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)}\right\|_{L^{\frac{m+1}{m}}(\mathbb{T})}^{\frac{m+1}{m}}\leq C_{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m+1)(\gamma-1)}.

Again, since we have
\displaystyle\frac{(m+1)s}{m}<\gamma+s
by (
122
), we conclude by Young’s inequality that

|I_{4}|\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)}dx+C_{\delta}.

Term I5.
Finally, we use the gradient bound to conclude for
s=m(\gamma-1)
that

\displaystyle\epsilon\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}(\rho_{\epsilon}^{s})dx\right|

\displaystyle=s\epsilon\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s}u_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}dx\right|=\epsilon\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\rho_{\epsilon}^{1-\frac{\gamma}{2}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{s-(m-1)\frac{(\gamma-1)}{2}}\Big)\Big((\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon})\rho_{\epsilon}^{\frac{\gamma}{2}-1}\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m-1)\frac{(\gamma-1)}{2}}u_{\epsilon}\Big)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{2-\gamma+2s-(m-1)(\gamma-1)}dx+C_{\delta}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\Big(\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma-2}|\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}|^{2}\Big)dx.

In the first integral we observe that for
s=m(\gamma-1)
, the exponent
{{2-\gamma+2s-(m-1)(\gamma-1)}=1+m(\gamma-1)}
.
In the second integral, we estimate
\rho_{\epsilon}^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}u_{\epsilon}^{2}\leq C_{m}(\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)}+u_{\epsilon}^{2m})
by using Young’s inequality with exponents
m
and
m/(m-1)
. Thanks to (
117
) we hence obtain,

\displaystyle\epsilon\left|\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}(\rho_{\epsilon}^{s})dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+m(\gamma-1)}ds+C_{m,\delta}\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)}+u_{\epsilon}^{2m}\Big)\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma-2}|\partial_{x}\rho_{\epsilon}|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\leq C_{m,\delta}+\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+m(\gamma-1)}dx.

Thus, for
\gamma>1
, we have

|I_{5}|\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)}+C_{m,\delta}.

Conclusion of the proof.
Combining all of the estimates of
I_{1}
through
I_{5}
in (
120
), we hence obtain:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{1+(m+1)(\gamma-1)}dx=\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)}dx\leq\delta\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma+m(\gamma-1)}dx+C_{m,\delta}.

Hence, taking
\delta
sufficiently small, we conclude the proof.
∎

7.2.
Additional estimates that are uniform in
\epsilon
.

Using the fundamental uniform entropy estimates in Proposition
7.1
, we can derive additional estimates that are uniform in
\epsilon
, which will be important in the limit passage as
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
. The first estimate is a uniform moment bound on the entropy and flux
(\eta_{m},H_{m})
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
be the
\epsilon
level statistically stationary solutions to the approximate
\epsilon
level problem in (
106
). Then, for every positive integer
m
, every
1\leq p<\infty
, and for all
t\geq 0
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})(t)|^{p}\leq C(m,p),\qquad\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|H_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})(t)|^{p}\leq C(m,p),

for a constant
C(m,p)
depending only on
m
and
p
, and
independent of both

\epsilon>0
and
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

This follows directly from combining the bounds (
111
) which hold for every positive integer
m
, obtained in Proposition
7.1
, with the algebraic identities in Lemma
1.1
.
∎

As a direct corollary, we can obtain uniform boundedness of the moments of the state variables, the density and momentum, independently of
\epsilon
.

Corollary 7.1
.

There exists a constant
C_{p}
depending only
p
(and independent of
\epsilon
and
t\geq 0
), such that for all
1\leq p<\infty
and for all
t\geq 0
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\rho_{\epsilon}(t)|^{p}\leq C_{s},\qquad\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|q_{\epsilon}(t)|^{p}\leq C_{p}.

Proof.

Using the algebraic bounds in Proposition
1.1
:

0\leq\rho u^{2m}+\rho^{1+m(\gamma-1)}\leq c_{m}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}),

for a positive constant
c_{m}
. So the following bounds follow immediately from the previous Proposition
7.2
for all
1\leq p<\infty
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\rho_{\epsilon}u_{\epsilon}^{2p}(t)|\leq C_{p},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\rho_{\epsilon}(t)|^{p}\leq C_{p},\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0,

where the constant
C_{p}
is independent of
t\geq 0
and
\epsilon>0
. Then, using Hölder’s inequality, for all
1\leq p<\infty
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\rho_{\epsilon}^{2}u_{\epsilon}^{2}|^{p}\leq C_{p}\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0,

for some uniform constant
C_{p}
independent of
\epsilon
and
t\geq 0
, which establishes the desired result.
∎

We next establish uniform local-in-time Hölder continuity bounds on the approximate solutions
\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}=(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
. To do this, we will use the uniform moment bounds in Proposition
7.2
and Corollary
7.1
to deduce an extension of the result in Proposition
7.1
to a maximum in time locally.

Proposition 7.3
.

For every
m\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C_{m}(t_{2}-t_{1})
that is independent of
\epsilon
such that

(123)

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})(t)\leq C_{m}(t_{2}-t_{1}).

In addition, there exists a constant
C(t_{2}-t_{1},p)
that is independent of
\epsilon
such that for all
1\leq p<\infty
:

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|^{p}\leq C(t_{2}-t_{1},p),\qquad\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|H_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|^{p}\leq C(t_{2}-t_{1},p),

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\rho_{\epsilon}(t)|^{p}\leq C(t_{2}-t_{1},p),\qquad\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|q_{\epsilon}(t)|^{p}\leq C(t_{2}-t_{1},p).

Proof.

It suffices to show the estimate (
123
), since the remaining estimates follow using the algebraic identities on the entropy in Proposition
1.1
and Lemma
1.1
, as in the proofs of Proposition
7.2
and Corollary
7.1
. So we prove the estimate in (
123
). To do this, we use the entropy formulation (
107
) with a test function
\varphi=1
, and we hence obtain for
t\geq t_{1}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})(t)=\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})(t_{1})

\displaystyle-\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\alpha q_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})+\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dW(t)

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G_{\epsilon}^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})+\epsilon\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle.

We then take a supremum over
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
and expectation, and obtain the following terms.

•

First, we note that by Proposition
7.1
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})(t_{1})\leq C_{m}.

•

Next, by the algebraic bounds in Proposition
1.1
and Young’s inequality, we have

|q_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|\leq C_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{(m-1)(\gamma-1)}u^{2})\leq C_{m}\rho(u^{2m}+\rho^{m(\gamma-1)})\leq C_{m}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}),

|\partial^{2}_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|\leq{A_{0}|\partial^{2}_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\rho_{\epsilon}^{2}|}\leq C_{m}\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}).

Therefore, we estimate:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\alpha q_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|+|\partial^{2}_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G^{2}_{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\left(\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}(t_{2}-t_{1}),

by Proposition
7.1
.

•

Recall that by stationarity (for example, by combining inequality (
115
) with Proposition
7.1
), we have that
\displaystyle\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle(t)\leq C_{m}
for a constant
C_{m}
that is independent of
t\geq 0
and
\epsilon
. Therefore,

\epsilon\mathbb{E}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\langle D^{2}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}\rangle\leq C_{m}(t_{2}-t_{1}).

•

Finally, using the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, we estimate that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dx\right)dW(t)\right|\leq\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G_{k}^{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dx\right)^{2}dt\right|^{1/2}.

Using the algebraic bounds in Proposition
1.1
:

\displaystyle|\partial_{q}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})G_{k}^{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}\alpha_{k}\rho_{\epsilon}(|u_{\epsilon}|^{2m-1}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{2(m-1)\theta}|u_{\epsilon}|)

\displaystyle\leq C_{m}\alpha_{k}\rho_{\epsilon}(1+|u_{\epsilon}|^{2m}+\rho_{\epsilon}^{m(\gamma-1)})\leq C_{m}\alpha_{k}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\Big).

Hence, using (
30
):

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})dW\right|\leq C_{m}A_{0}\mathbb{E}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big[\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\Big)^{2}+\Big(\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\Big)^{2}\Big]\leq C_{m}(t_{2}-t_{1}),

by the uniform moment bounds on the entropies in Proposition
7.2
.

Hence, we deduce that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\leq C_{m}\Big(1+(t_{2}-t_{1})\Big).

∎

Finally, we conclude this section on uniform in
\epsilon
bounds on the statistically stationary solutions by deriving the following uniform bound on Hölder continuity in time, for the approximate statistically stationary solutions
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
.

Proposition 7.4
.

For each
\beta\in(0,1/4)
and every
T>0
, there exists a constant
C_{\beta,T}
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\mathbb{E}\|(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})\|_{C^{\beta}(0,T;H^{-2}(\mathbb{T}))}\leq C_{\beta,T},

where
H^{-2}(\mathbb{T})
denotes the dual of
H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
.

Proof.

We can establish this bound by using the Kolmogorov continuity criterion. Namely, it suffices to show that for each deterministic
\varphi\in H^{2}(\mathbb{T})
with
\|\varphi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 1
, and for all times
0\leq t_{1}\leq t_{2}\leq T
:

(124)

\mathbb{E}|\langle\rho_{\epsilon}(t_{2})-\rho_{\epsilon}(t_{1}),\varphi\rangle|^{4}\leq C_{T}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{2},\qquad\mathbb{E}|\langle q_{\epsilon}(t_{2})-q_{\epsilon}(t_{1}),\varphi\rangle|^{4}\leq C_{T}|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{2}.

Using the weak formulation (which follows from the entropy equality (
107
) for the approximate system (
106
)):

\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\rho_{\epsilon}(t_{2})-\rho_{\epsilon}(t_{1})\Big)\varphi\right|^{4}\leq C\left(\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}q_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}\varphi\right|^{4}+\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi\right|^{4}\right)\leq C|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{4}\leq CT^{2}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2},

using Proposition
7.3
and the fact that
\|\varphi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq 1
. Similarly, we estimate using the weak formulation, that

\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(q_{\epsilon}(t_{2})-q_{\epsilon}(t_{1})\Big)\varphi\right|^{4}\\
\leq C\Bigg(\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{q_{\epsilon}^{2}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}\partial_{x}\varphi\right|^{4}+\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\kappa\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\partial_{x}\varphi\right|^{4}+\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\epsilon q_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi\right|^{4}+\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon}(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})\varphi dxdW_{\epsilon}(t)\right|^{4}\Bigg).

We can then estimate, using Proposition
7.3
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{q_{\epsilon}^{2}}{\rho_{\epsilon}}\partial_{x}\varphi\right|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\|\eta_{1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\right|^{4}\leq|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{4}\cdot\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\eta_{1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|^{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{4}\left(\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\eta_{1}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})|^{4}\right)\leq C_{T}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{4}.

We can similarly use the higher moment bounds on the supremum in time of
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
in Proposition
7.3
to deduce that

\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\kappa\rho_{\epsilon}^{\gamma}\partial_{x}\varphi\right|^{4}+\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\epsilon q_{\epsilon}\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi\right|^{4}\leq C_{T}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{4}.

Finally, we use the BDG inequality, (
30
), (
31
), and
\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{T})}\leq C
, to estimate:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\boldsymbol{\Phi}^{\epsilon}(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})\varphi dxdW_{\epsilon}(t)\right|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}G_{k}^{\epsilon}(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})\varphi dx\right)^{2}dt\right|^{2}\leq C\mathbb{E}\left(\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|G_{k}^{\epsilon}(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})|^{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq CA_{0}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\rho_{\epsilon}^{2}\leq C_{T}|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2}.

This establishes both estimates in (
124
), and completes the proof of the proposition via the Kolmogorov continuity criterion.
∎

8.
Passage to the limit:
\epsilon_{N}\to 0

We now carry out the final limit passage in the stationary solutions
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon},W_{\epsilon})
as
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
. Note that the laws of
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
are tight in
C_{loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{+};H^{-3}(\mathbb{T}))
by the uniform equicontinuity estimate in Proposition
7.4
. However, having almost sure strong convergence in the very weak space
C(0,T;H^{-3}(\mathbb{T}))
is not enough to pass to the limit in the approximate entropy equality (
107
), in order to obtain the entropy inequality (
11
) for the limiting solution
(\rho,q)
to the original problem. In particular, to pass to the limit as
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
in the entropy equality (
107
), we must be able to pass to the limit in the composition of continuous functions with the solution itself, as we must pass to the limit in
\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})
and
H(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})
for entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
. To do this, we will appeal to theory of
Young measures
, which gives a way of passing to the limit in compositions with continuous functions, at the price of making the solution measure-valued, so at every point
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
, the solution is a measure on the state space
[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
for the density and momentum. However, we will be able to reduce the measure-valued limit to an actual real-valued function using a reduction of Young measure argument that is standard in the literature for the compressible isentropic Euler equations in 1D. See Section 5.2 in
[
4
]
and Section I.5 in
[
35
]
. This will then allow us to pass to the limit as
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
in the entropy equality and complete the proof.

In this section, we will hence give an exposition on (random) Young measures in the context of the current problem. Then, we will use these results to apply the Skorohod representation theorem to the approximate solutions in
\epsilon
to obtain a limiting (measure-valued) solution in the limit. We will then appeal to usual reduction of Young measure arguments for the compressible isentropic Euler equations, which involves using a functional equation for the limiting solution, in order to show that our limiting stationary solution to the original problem is genuinely function-valued. Finally, we pass to the limit in the entropy equality as
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
to conclude the proof, and hence obtain a statistically stationary weak martingale solution to the original problem (
1
).

8.1.
An exposition on Young measures

Let
(X,\lambda)
be a
\sigma
-finite measure space, and let
(E,\mathcal{B}(E))
be a topological space
E
equipped with the Borel
\sigma
-algebra, namely the
\sigma
-algebra generated by all open and closed sets in the topology of
E
. Then, recall that a
measurable function

f:X\to E
is a function for which
f^{-1}(A)
is a measurable subset of
X
for every measurable subset
A\subset E
. For concreteness and to elucidate the notation, we remark that the approximate solutions
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
are measurable functions from
X=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
(spacetime) to
E=[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
(the set of admissible values for the density and momentum). We hence make the distinction between a measurable function and a Young measure, which unlike a genuine real-valued function, is probability measure-valued at each point
x\in X
. Specifically, we have the following definition.

Definition 8.1
.

A
Young measure
is a map
\nu:X\to\mathcal{P}(E)
, where
\mathcal{P}(E)
is the set of probability measures on
E
equipped with the weak-star topology. A Young measure is required to be
weakly-star measurable
in the sense that for every continuous and bounded function
\varphi\in C_{b}(E)
, the function

f_{\varphi}(x)=\langle\varphi,\nu_{x}\rangle:=\int_{E}\varphi(p)d\nu_{x}(p)

is a measurable map from
X
to
E
, where we denote the (probability measure) value of
\nu
at the point
x\in X
by
\nu_{x}
.

For more information, we direct the reader to Sections 4.1 and 4.2 of
[
4
]
.

Note that a Young measure is a generalization of a pointwise-defined function. In the case where
f:X\to E
is a pointwise-defined measurable function, we can associate to
f
the corresponding Young measure
\nu^{f}
such that:

\nu^{f}_{x}=\delta_{f(x)},

where
\delta_{f(x)}
is a Dirac-delta probability measure on
E
centered at
f(x)\in E
. In this case, we can view the action of integrating against the Young measure as function composition, since

\langle\varphi,\nu^{f}_{x}\rangle=\int_{E}\varphi(p)d\delta_{f(x)}(p)=\varphi(f(x)),\qquad\text{ for all }x\in X\text{ and }\varphi\in C_{b}(E).

We can define the space of Young measures
\mathcal{V}
on the space
X
taking values in
\mathcal{P}(E)
, and we equip this space with the (vague) weak-star topology of convergence. Namely, we have the following definition of convergence.

Definition 8.2
.

A
sequence of Young measure
\{\nu_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}:X\to\mathcal{P}(E)
converges weakly-star to a limiting Young measure
\nu
if

\int_{X}\psi(x)\int_{E}\varphi(p)d\nu_{x}(p)d\lambda(x)\to\int_{X}\psi(x)\int_{E}\varphi(p)d\nu_{x}(p)d\lambda(x),\quad\text{ for all }\varphi\in C_{b}(E)\text{ and }\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(X).

We refer the reader to Section 2.8 of
[
7
]
for more information about Young measures, and for more exposition about the ideas above.

Finally, we note that since we are considering a stochastic problem, we must also consider a notion of random Young measures.

Definition 8.3
.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space. A
random (probabilistic) Young measure
is a measurable map from
\Omega\to\mathcal{V}
, where
\mathcal{V}
is the space of Young measures on
X
taking values in
\mathcal{P}(E)
, equipped with the (vague) weak-star topology of convergence.

Next, we will apply the theory of probabilistic Young measures to the current problem, in terms of expressing the approximate solutions
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
in terms of Young measures. For
\epsilon>0
and any
\omega\in\Omega
, these are genuine pointwise-defined functions from
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
to
(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})\in[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
. For the purpose of using probabilistic Young measures, it will often be more convenient to consider these approximate solutions in terms of the density and the
fluid velocity
(instead of the fluid momentum), so that the approximate solutions are
(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})
, since the entropy functions
\eta_{m}
are polynomials in
\rho
and
u
(rather than
\rho
and
q
).

A technical difficulty here is defining the fluid velocity,
u_{\epsilon}=q_{\epsilon}/\rho_{\epsilon}
when there is vacuum
\rho_{\epsilon}=0
. For this purpose, we will define the phase space
[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
with a different topology that identifies all points with
\{\rho=0\}
as the same. This is in the spirit of Section 2.3 in
[
33
]
, and we introduce the following definitions that follow the notation of
[
33
]
.

Definition 8.4
.

Let
H:=(0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
be equipped with the usual topology of Euclidean space (the subspace topology). Consider
\overline{\mathcal{H}}:=[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
as a compactification of
H
, and define the space
C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
of functions
f
on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
such that:

•

f
is a continuous function on
[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
in the usual sense.

•

f
is constant along the vacuum set
V:=\{(\rho,u):\rho=0\}
.

•

The function
f_{\infty}(z):=\lim_{r\to\infty}f(rz)
is a bounded continuous function on
S^{1}\cap([0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}})
, where
S^{1}
is the unit circle in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.

We then endow the space
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
with the following topology of convergence, where
x_{n}\to x
in
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
if
f(x_{n})\to f(x)
for all
f\in C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
. Note that with this topology,
C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
coincides with the continuous bounded functions on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
.

Remark 8.1
.

This compactification
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
can be thought of as a closed half 2-sphere with the space
H
being the open half 2-sphere, the vacuum set
V
, being reduced to a single point on the circular boundary of the half 2-sphere, and all upper half plane points at infinity being the rest of the circular boundary of the half 2-sphere. Hence, topologically, the compactification reduces all points in
V
to be the “same” point, which makes sense, as points in
V
are indistinguishable (since the fluid velocity
u
does not have a well-defined value and momentum is 0 at vacuum).

The Young measures for the approximate solutions
(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})
will be defined on
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
, and will take values in
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
. In the next subsection, we explicitly define these Young measures and then state results about compactness criteria for these approximate Young measures.

8.2.
Young measures for approximate solutions and compactness results

We consider the approximate solutions
(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})
for each parameter
\epsilon>0
, defined for
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
. These are random (genuinely pointwise-defined) functions taking values in
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
, where we recall that vacuum points with
\rho=0
in
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
are considered to be indistinguishable. Thus, we can define an associated probabilistic Young measure for each approximate solution
(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})
, by

\nu_{\epsilon}:=\delta_{(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})}.

These are random Young measures on the sigma-finite measure space
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
, with respect to the (range) space
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
. The goal will be to show that this sequence of random Young measures is tight, as random processes taking values in the space
\mathcal{V}
of Young measures from
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
to
{\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})}
.

This requires compactness results for Young measures. Namely, recall that (deterministic) Young measures
\nu_{n}
converge to
\nu
in the (vague) weak-star topology of
\mathcal{V}
if

(125)

\int_{[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}}\psi(t,x)\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(p)d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\to\int_{[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}}\psi(t,x)\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(p)d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x),\\
\qquad\text{ for all }\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T})\text{ and }\varphi\in C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}}),

where we recall the definition of
C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
from Definition
8.4
. We have the following result on compactness of deterministic Young measures, adapted to the specific function spaces and range spaces we are considering.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\{C_{m}\}_{m=1}^{\infty}
be a monotonically increasing sequence of positive constants. The set of Young measures from
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
with range space
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
satisfying:

(126)

\bigcap_{m=1}^{\infty}\left\{\nu\in\mathcal{V}:\int_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\eta_{E}(p)d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\leq C_{m}\right\},

where
\eta_{E}(\rho,u):=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\rho u^{2}+\frac{\kappa}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma}
is the energy functional (the first entropy), is compact in
\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

Consider a sequence
\{\nu_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of Young measures in the set (
126
), and consider the restriction of the Young measures to each set
[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}
, which we denote by
\nu_{n}|_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}
. We claim that for each fixed
m
, there exists a subsequence along which
\nu_{n_{k}}|_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}
converges weakly-star to some
\nu_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}
in the sense that

(127)

\int_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}\psi(t,x)\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(p)d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\to\int_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}\psi(t,x)\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(p)d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x),\\
\text{ for all }\psi\in C([0,m]\times\mathbb{T})\text{ and }\varphi\in C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}}).

To show this, it suffices by Prokhorov’s theorem to show that the
\lambda\ltimes\nu_{n}|_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}
, are tight as finite measures on
[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}\times\overline{\mathcal{H}}
. To do this, consider the set:

K_{R}:=\eta_{E}^{-1}([0,R])=\{(\rho,u)\in\overline{\mathcal{H}}:0\leq\eta_{E}(\rho,u)\leq R\}.

Note that this set
K_{R}
is compact in
\overline{\mathcal{H}}
under the topology of
K_{R}
defined in Definition
8.4
(even though
K_{R}
is not compact with respect to the usual subspace topology of
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
since
\eta_{E}(0,u)=0
for all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
). Hence, by (
126
):

\lambda\ltimes\nu_{n}|_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}\Big(([0,m]\times\mathbb{T}\times K_{R})^{c}\Big)\leq\frac{C_{m}}{R},

which shows tightness of the measures
\lambda\ltimes\nu_{n}|_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}
, and hence shows that along a subsequence,
\lambda\ltimes\nu_{n_{k}}|_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}
converges to some limiting
\lambda\ltimes\nu|_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}
weakly-star in the sense of (
127
).

Hence, we can show compactness of the original set (
126
) in the original sigma finite (but infinite measure) space of
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
by using a diagonalization argument, namely, extract a convergence subsequence converging as Young measures on
[0,1]\times[0,\infty)
, and then in general successively refine the subsequence to get convergence on larger and larger domains
[0,m]\times[0,\infty)
. We can use a diagonalization procedure to extract a convergent subsequence for the whole space, and it is easy to see using the definition of convergence (
127
) that the limiting Young measures on
[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}
must agree for different values of
m
on their overlap. This concludes the proof.
∎

We can extend this to a tightness result for
probabilistic
Young measures on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
taking values in
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\{\nu_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of probabilistic Young measures on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
taking values in
\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
, satisfying for all positive integers
m
:

\mathbb{E}\int_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\eta_{E}(p)d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\leq\alpha_{m},

for some positive constants
\alpha_{m}
. Then, the sequence
\{\nu_{n}\}
is tight in
\mathcal{V}
.

Proof.

This follows by combining the deterministic compactness result in Proposition
8.1
with Chebychev’s inequality. Consider an arbitrary
\varepsilon>0
. For each
m
, we define
C_{m}:=\alpha_{m}2^{m}\varepsilon^{-1}
so that

\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\eta_{E}(p)d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p){d\lambda(t,x)}>C_{m}\right)<\varepsilon 2^{-m}\qquad\text{ for all positive integers }m\text{ and }n,

by Chebychev’s inequality. Then, the set

K_{\varepsilon}:={\bigcap_{m=1}^{\infty}}\left\{\nu\in\mathcal{V}:\int_{[0,m]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\eta_{E}(p)d\nu_{t,x}(t,x){d\lambda(x,t)}\leq C_{m}\right\}

is a compact set in
\mathcal{V}
by Proposition
8.1
, and furthermore,

\mathbb{P}(\nu_{n}\in K_{\varepsilon}^{c})\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\varepsilon 2^{-m}=\varepsilon.

This establishes the tightness claim.
∎

Finally, we end this section by discussing the convergence of continuous and bounded functionals of Young measures, in both the deterministic and stochastic settings, which is referred to as
momentum convergence
. Specifically, we have the following deterministic result on momentum convergence.

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\nu_{n}\to\nu
in
\mathcal{V}
be a sequence of Young measures
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}\to\mathcal{P}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
satisfying

(128)

\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}|\eta(p)|^{s}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\leq C_{T},

for some finite
T>0
, and for some continuous and bounded function
\eta:[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
(with respect to the usual Euclidean topology) satisfying one of the following two cases:

•

Case 1.
For all
(\rho,u)\in(0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lim_{r\to\infty}\eta(r(\rho,u))
is either
-\infty
,
0
, or
\infty
, and
\eta(0,u)=0
for all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

Case 2.

\eta\in C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
in the sense of Definition
8.4
.

Then,

(129)

\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}|\eta(p)|^{s}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\leq C_{T},

for the same constant
C_{T}
, and furthermore, for all
\varphi\in L^{\frac{s}{s-r}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
and
1\leq r<s
:

(130)

\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(t,x)(\eta(p))^{r}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\to\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(t,x)(\eta(p))^{r}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x).

Proof.

We follow the proof of Proposition 4.3 in
[
4
]
, and make some additional comments. First, we use the fact that
\nu_{n}\to\nu
in
\mathcal{V}
, in the sense of (
125
). This immediately gives us convergence against functionals in
C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
in the sense of Definition
8.4
, namely the result for Case 2.
However,
\eta
might not be in
C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
, in the first case above where
\lim_{r\to\infty}\eta(r(\rho,u))=-\infty,0,\infty
for each
(\rho,u)\in(0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
and
\eta(0,u)=0
for all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

However, using a radially symmetric compactly supported smooth truncation function
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
that is decreasing radially, even, with
\chi(z)=1
on
[-1,1]
and
\chi(z)=0
for
|z|\geq 2
, we note that
\chi_{R}(\eta(p))\eta(p)
for
\chi_{R}(z):=\chi(p/R)
is indeed in
C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
, since the limit radially at infinity is just zero in all directions in the first case above for
\eta
. Hence, for any
1\leq r<s
:

(131)

\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(t,x)\chi_{R}(\eta(p))(\eta(p))^{r}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\to\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(t,x)\chi_{R}(p)(\eta(p))^{r}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x),\\
\qquad\text{ for all $R$},

and setting
\varphi=1
:

(132)

\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\chi_{R}(\eta(p))|\eta(p)|^{s}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\to\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\chi_{R}(p)|\eta(p)|^{s}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x),\quad\text{ for all $R$}.

So using (
128
) and (
132
), by monotone convergence:

\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}|\eta(p)|^{r}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\chi_{R}(\eta(p))|\eta(p)|^{r}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\leq C_{T},

which establishes (
129
). To show (
130
), we use (
131
). We can estimate that for
1\leq r<s
:

\displaystyle\left|\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(t,x)(1-\chi_{R}(\eta(p)))(\eta(p))^{r}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{R^{s-r}}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}|\varphi(t,x)|\cdot|\eta(p)|^{s}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\to 0,\quad{\text{ as }R\to\infty},

uniformly in
n
, by the given bounds and the fact that
\varphi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
. A similar convergence holds for the limiting Young measure
\nu
, which gives us the desired convergence (
130
) for
\varphi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
, using (
128
) and (
129
). We can then extend the convergence (
130
) to
\varphi\in L^{\frac{s}{s-r}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
using a density argument.
∎

We can extend this momentum convergence result to probabilistic Young measures, as follows.

Proposition 8.4
.

Let
\nu_{n}\to\nu
almost surely, as probabilistic Young measures from
[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}
to
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
, satisfying the following uniform bound:

\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\eta(p))^{s}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\leq C_{T},

for some finite
T>0
and for some continuous bounded function
\eta:[0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that either

•

Case 1.
For all
(\rho,u)\in(0,\infty)\times{\mathbb{R}}
,
\lim_{r\to\infty}\eta(r(\rho,u))
is either
-\infty
,
0
, or
\infty
, and
\eta(0,u)=0
for all
u\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

•

Case 2.

\eta\in C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
in the sense of Definition
8.4
.

Then, for the same constant
C_{T}
as above:

\mathbb{E}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}(\eta(p))^{s}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\leq C_{T},

and for all
\varphi\in L^{\frac{s}{s-r}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
and for all
1\leq r<s
and
1\leq\delta<s/r
:

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left|\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(t,x)(\eta(p))^{r}d(\nu_{n})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)-\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\varphi(t,x)(\eta(p))^{r}d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\right|^{\delta}=0.

Proof.

We refer the reader to the proof of Proposition 4.5 in
[
4
]
.
∎

8.3.
The Skorohod argument for the
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
limit passage

Next, we pass to the limit in the
\epsilon
-level approximate solutions using a Skorohod representation theorem argument. We define the phase space:

\mathcal{X}:=\mathcal{V}\times C_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{-3}(\mathbb{T}))\cap(C_{w,loc}([0,\infty);L^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))\times C_{w,loc}([0,\infty);L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\mathbb{T})))\times C_{loc}([0,\infty);\mathcal{U}),

where
\mathcal{V}
is the space of Young measures on
[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
taking values in
\mathcal{P}(\bar{\mathcal{H}})
, and we consider the laws
\mu_{\epsilon}
of the approximate solutions

(\nu_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},W_{\epsilon}),\qquad\text{ for }\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}:=(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon}),

in the phase space
\mathcal{X}
. Here we recall that
\nu_{\epsilon}=\delta_{(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})}
. We claim that we have the following tightness result.

Proposition 8.5
.

The laws
\{\mu_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
are tight as probability measures on the phase space
\mathcal{X}
.

Proof.

By the result in Proposition
7.3
, we have that

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{m}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{E}(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})dxdt\leq C_{m},

for some constant
C_{m}
, for each positive integer
m
, since
{{\eta_{1}=2\eta_{E}}}
. Since the Young measure for the approximate solution is
\nu_{\epsilon}=\delta_{(\rho_{\epsilon},u_{\epsilon})}
, we conclude by Proposition
8.2
that the laws of the probabilistic Young measures
\{\nu_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
are tight in
\mathcal{V}
. Furthermore, by the compact embedding

C^{\alpha}_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{-2}(\mathbb{T}))\subset\subset C_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{-3}(\mathbb{T})),

which is a consequence of the Arzela-Ascoli compactness theorem, and the following embedding stated in Theorem 1.8.5 in
[
7
]
:

(133)

\displaystyle C_{loc}^{\alpha}([0,\infty);H^{-2}(\mathbb{T}))\cap L^{\infty}([0,\infty);L^{p}(\mathbb{T}))\subset\subset C_{w,loc}([0,\infty);L^{p}(\mathbb{T})),\quad\text{for any }\alpha>0,1<p<\infty.

we have tightness of the laws of
\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon}:=(\rho_{\epsilon},q_{\epsilon})
in
C_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{-3}(\mathbb{T}))\cap(C_{w,loc}([0,\infty);L^{\gamma}(\mathbb{T}))\times C_{w,loc}([0,\infty);L^{\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+1}}(\mathbb{T})))
by the estimate in Proposition
7.4
and Corollary
7.1
. The tightness of the laws of
\{W_{\epsilon}\}_{\epsilon>0}
in
C_{loc}([0,\infty);\mathcal{U}))
is immediate.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

Note that due to the embedding (
133
), we can construct statistically stationary solutions in the state space
L^{p}(\mathbb{T})\times L^{p}(\mathbb{T})
for any
1<p<\infty
, as described in Remark
1.3
.

The tightness result in Proposition
8.5
allows us to use the Skorohod representation theorem since the path space
\mathcal{X}
is a Jakubowski space.

Proposition 8.6
.

There exists a probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
and random variables
(\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon},\tilde{W}_{\epsilon})
and
({\nu},{\boldsymbol{U}},{W})
taking values in
\mathcal{X}
, where we will denote
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon}:=(\tilde{\rho}_{\epsilon},\tilde{q}_{\epsilon})
and
{\boldsymbol{U}}:=({\rho},{q})
, such that

(134)

(\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon},\tilde{W}_{\epsilon})=_{d}(\nu_{\epsilon},\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon},W_{\epsilon}),

and

(\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon},\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon},\tilde{W}_{\epsilon})\to({\nu},{\boldsymbol{U}},{W}),\qquad\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\text{-almost surely in the topology of $\mathcal{X}$}.

Furthermore, the limiting process
\{{W}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a
\mathcal{U}
-Wiener process with respect to the filtration
\{{\mathcal{F}}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined by:

{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\sigma\{\boldsymbol{U}(s),{W}(s):0\leq s\leq t\}.

Using the Young measures will allow us to pass to the limit in
\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})
to
\eta({\boldsymbol{U}})
, but the issue is that we do not know that the limiting solution
({\rho},{q})
is function-valued, since it is represented either by a limiting Young measure
{\nu}
, or a very weak distributional space
C_{loc}([0,\infty);H^{-3}(\mathbb{T}))
. We therefore will need to carry out a
reduction of the Young measure
, which is an argument in which we will show that the limiting Young measure corresponds to a genuine function, rather than a measure-valued solution.

8.4.
Reduction of the Young measure

In this subsection, we will reduce the limiting Young measure
{\nu}
to a Dirac mass, which means that we will show that

\text{this Young measure $\nu$ corresponds to a genuine function, except potentially when }{\rho}=0.
