In this sense, the limiting density and momentum
({\rho},{q})
is function valued, since we recall that the Young measure is defined for density and velocity, and hence, the momentum
{q}
is unambiguously equal to zero in the vacuum (even if the value of
{u}
is not necessarily well-defined).

The main ingredient in the argument for the reduction of the Young measure is the following
key functional equation
, which holds for all entropy flux pairs
(\eta,H)
and
(\hat{\eta},\hat{H})
arising from subquadratic
g\in\mathcal{G}
(see Definition
1.1
), and almost surely for all almost every
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
:

(135)

\langle\eta,{\nu}\rangle\langle\hat{H},{\nu}\rangle-\langle\hat{\eta},{\nu}\rangle\langle H,{\nu}\rangle=\langle\eta\hat{H}-\hat{\eta}H,{\nu}\rangle,\qquad\text{ where }\langle f,{\nu}\rangle:=\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}f(p)d{\nu}_{t,x}(p).

This functional equation is the key ingredient for an argument for the reduction of the Young measure, which will show that the Young measure is a Dirac delta function at each
(t,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
almost surely, except potentially on vacuum when
\rho(t,x)=0
. Since this argument is a standard argument in the literature, we do not provide the argument here and instead refer the reader to Section 5.2 in
[
4
]
, and also Section I.5 in
[
35
]
(see also the discussion on pg. 604 in
[
35
]
).

We make a few comments on how the functional equation (
135
) is obtained. Since the approximate Young measures
\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon}
are Dirac-delta functions (namely, they arise from the approximate statistically stationary solutions
(\tilde{\rho}_{\epsilon},\tilde{q}_{\epsilon})
which are function valued), the functional equation (
135
) holds trivially for the approximate Young measures:

(136)

\langle\eta,\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon}(t,x)\rangle\langle\hat{H},\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon}(t,x)\rangle-\langle\hat{\eta},\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon}(t,x)\rangle\langle H,\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon}(t,x)\rangle=\langle\eta\hat{H}-\hat{\eta}H,\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon}(t,x)\rangle.

Hence, we can obtain (
135
) by passing to the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
in (
136
), and this can be done using the div-curl lemma and Murat’s lemma, exactly as in Section 5.1 in
[
4
]
, since the only uniform in
\epsilon
estimates required for this argument for obtaining (
135
), are the estimates that we have established in Proposition
7.1
. Hence, we will not provide the details here.

8.5.
The limiting martingale solution

Finally, we take the limit as
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
in the approximate entropy equality (
107
) at the
\epsilon
level. Note that since
\eta
is convex and the test functions
\varphi(x)\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and
\psi(t)\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,\infty)
in the entropy equality are nonnegative, we have that

\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\langle D^{2}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon},\partial_{x}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon}\rangle\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt\geq 0,

and hence, we have that
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely:

(137)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon}(t))\varphi(x)dx\right)\partial_{t}\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}H(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}\varphi(x)\right)\psi(t)dt\\
-\alpha\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}q_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\Phi(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)d\tilde{W}_{\epsilon}(t)\\
+\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})G^{2}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)dt+\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi dx\right)\psi(t)dt\geq 0,

for all
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T})
and
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,\infty)
, with
\varphi,\psi\geq 0
.

Our goal is to pass to the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
in the entropy inequality (
137
). To do this, we combine the results on higher moment entropy bounds in Proposition
7.2
with the probabilistic momentum convergence results in Proposition
8.4
. As of now, we already know after the reduction of the Young measure, the convergence (
134
), and the definition of (vague) convergence of Young measures in Definition
8.2
that

\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\psi(t,x)S(p)d(\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon})_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x)\to\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\psi(t,x)S(p)d\nu_{t,x}(p)d\lambda(t,x),

for all
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
and
S\in C_{b}(\overline{\mathcal{H}})
in the sense of Definition
8.4
. We want to extend this convergence to nonlinear continuous functions of the form found in (
137
), such as those which are potentially unbounded. In particular, we have the following convergence result.

Proposition 8.7
.

For
\eta_{m}
defined for
g(z)=z^{2m}
via (
9
),
\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\to\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})
in
L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
for all
T>0
.

Proof.

We follow the approach of the analogous result in Proposition 5.11 in
[
4
]
. First, note that since the Young measures
\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon}
correspond to genuine functions and since the reduction of Young measure argument shows that
\nu
is function-valued (except potentially on vacuum which is fine, since
\eta_{m}
is zero on vacuum), we have that

\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})=\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\eta_{m}(p)d(\tilde{\nu}_{\epsilon})_{t,x}(p),\qquad\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})=\int_{\overline{\mathcal{H}}}\eta_{m}(p)d\nu_{t,x}(p).

Note that by the entropy moment bounds in Proposition
7.2
, we have that for all
1\leq s<\infty
:

(138)

{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}|\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})|^{s}dxdt\leq C_{T},

independently of
\epsilon
. Note that by the algebraic estimates in Proposition
1.1
,
\eta_{m}
satisfies Case 1 in Proposition
8.4
. Hence, by Proposition
8.4
, we conclude that for all
\varphi\in L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})

{\mathbb{E}}\left|\int_{[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\varphi(t,x)dxdt-\int_{[0,T]}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\varphi(t,x)dxdt\right|^{2}\to 0,

and by setting
\varphi=1
and
r=2
in Proposition
8.4
:

{\mathbb{E}}\Big(\|\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\|_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}^{2}-\|\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{2}([0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}^{2}\Big)\to 0.

So we conclude that
\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\rightharpoonup\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})
weakly in
L^{2}({\tilde{\Omega}\times}[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
, and we have convergence of the norms

\displaystyle\|\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\|_{L^{2}({\tilde{\Omega}\times}[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}\to\|\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\|_{L^{2}({\tilde{\Omega}\times}[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})}
. Hence, by combining weak convergence with convergence of the norms:

\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\to\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U}),\qquad\text{ strongly in $L^{2}({\tilde{\Omega}\times}[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})$. }

∎

We also have almost everywhere convergence of the approximate solutions
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon}
along a subsequence.

Proposition 8.8
.

Along a subsequence
\{\epsilon_{N_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
, we have that
(\tilde{\rho}_{\epsilon},\tilde{q}_{\epsilon})\to(\rho,q)
almost everywhere on
\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{T}
.

Proof.

Note that the proof in Proposition
8.7
also works to show that for the functions
\eta(\rho u)=\rho
and
\eta(\rho,u)=\rho u
, we have that
\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\to\eta(\boldsymbol{U})
in
L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})
for all
T>0
. This is because we still have uniform bounds:

{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\tilde{\rho}_{\epsilon}+|\tilde{\rho}_{\epsilon}\tilde{u}_{\epsilon}|\Big)^{s}dxdt\leq C_{s,T},

for all
1\leq s<\infty
, using
\rho+|\rho u|\leq C\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{1}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big)
and Proposition
7.2
. This establishes the result, since convergence in
L^{p}
for
1\leq p<\infty
implies convergence almost everywhere along a subsequence.
∎

Now, we have all of the necessary ingredients needed to pass to the limit as
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
in the entropy inequality (
137
). The goal is to obtain the limiting entropy inequality (
11
), which is expected to hold for all entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
generated by all subpolynomial functions
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proposition 8.9
.

The entropy inequality (
11
) holds
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely for the limiting solution
(\rho,q)
and for all entropy-flux pairs
(\eta,H)
generated from
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
.

Proof.

We pass to the limit in each term in the approximate
\epsilon
-level entropy inequality (
137
). To do this, consider some
(\eta,H)
generated by
g\in\tilde{\mathcal{G}}
satisfying (
10
) for some positive integer
m
, and recall the algebraic bounds in Proposition
1.4
and
1.5
:

|q\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),\qquad|G(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),

(139)

|G^{2}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{q}^{2}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m-1}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big),\qquad|H(\boldsymbol{U})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m+1}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big).

By the continuity of
\eta
and its derivatives,
H
, and
\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{U})
, we hence deduce that for all
T>0
:

(140)

S(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\to S(\boldsymbol{U})\quad\text{in $L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T})$ },\ \ \text{for $S=\{q\partial_{q}\eta,\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial_{q}\eta,\rho\partial_{q}\eta,\boldsymbol{G}^{2}(\boldsymbol{U})\partial^{2}_{q}\eta,H\}$}.

This follows by the generalized dominated convergence theorem (see Theorem 11 in Section 4.4 of
[
42
]
), combined with the almost everywhere convergence in Proposition
8.8
, and the convergence in Proposition
8.7
combined with the algebraic bounds (
139
). For example, for
q\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})
, we note that
|q\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})|\leq C_{g}\Big(\eta_{0}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})+\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\Big)
,

{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\eta_{0}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})+\eta_{m}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\Big)^{2}dxdt\to{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\eta_{0}(\boldsymbol{U})+\eta_{m}(\boldsymbol{U})\Big)^{2}dxdt,

and
|q\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})-q\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})|\to 0
almost everywhere on
\tilde{\Omega}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{T}
by Proposition
8.8
.

The convergence in (
140
) is sufficient to pass to the limit in the terms in the approximate entropy inequality (
137
). We only explicitly comment on the passage of the stochastic integral term here. Consider some
\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(0,\infty)
, and note that it has support in
[0,T]
for sufficiently large
T
. By standard results on convergence of stochastic integrals (see
[
2
]
, Lemma 2.1 in
[
17
]
, and Lemma 2.6.6 in
[
7
]
), it suffices to show that

(141)

\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\boldsymbol{G}^{\epsilon}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t)\to\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{U})\varphi(x)dx\right)\psi(t),\text{ in probability in $L^{2}(0,T)$}.

To show this, note that

{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\boldsymbol{U})-\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\boldsymbol{\Phi}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\Big)\varphi(x)dx\right)^{2}(\psi(t))^{2}dt\\
\leq\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T)}^{2}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T})}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U})\boldsymbol{G}(\boldsymbol{U})-\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\boldsymbol{G}^{\epsilon}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\Big)^{2}dxdt\to 0,

as
\epsilon\to 0
. This follows by (
140
) and the fact that
\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{T}}\Big(\partial_{q}\eta(\boldsymbol{U}_{\epsilon})(\boldsymbol{G}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})-\boldsymbol{G}^{\epsilon}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})\Big)^{2}\to 0
by generalized dominated convergence with the dominating function
\tilde{\rho}_{\epsilon}\partial_{q}\eta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\epsilon})
using (
5
) and (
36
) (see Theorem 11 of Section 4.4 in
[
42
]
). This completes the proof of the main theorem in Theorem
1.1
.
∎
